# RAYMOND’S LITTLE
## WORK ON THE KABBALISTIC
### *Lecture, or an introduction*
### *to all*
### *sciences.*

Since indeed all men possess an innate natural desire to know and understand the truth in all knowable things—as Aristotle <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE) was the foundational figure for medieval and Renaissance logic and science.</note> testifies in the first book of his *Metaphysics* <note>original: "primo Metaphysicæ"</note>—it follows that whatever possesses the capacity for wonder as a whole species likewise possesses a natural desire to know and understand the truth in all things that can be known. And since all men possess this capacity for wonder as a whole species, it therefore follows that they desire this knowledge.

Therefore, desiring to be perfected in this task, it is necessary to search for the method of attempting it. Hence, the higher a subject is, the more worthy it is of being known, since it is more true because of its proximity to the most True <note>In Lullian philosophy, "the most True" refers to God, the source of all truth and being.</note>, for...

<summary>This page opens the main text by citing Aristotle's famous principle that all humans naturally desire knowledge. It establishes the philosophical merit of the book's "Kabbalistic" method as a way to reach higher, more divine truths.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Metaphysics, Ramon Llull, epistemology, desire for knowledge, Kabbalah, Renaissance logic, truth</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "Auditus Kabbalisticus," transitioning from the general desire for knowledge to the specific metaphysical foundations of Ramon Llull’s system. The previous page ended with "for...", leading directly into the explanation of truth and being.</meta>

...on which account we believe truth to be the proper object of the soul. And since all truth presupposes <term>Being</term> <note>original: "esse." In this philosophical context, "Being" refers to the fundamental existence of anything that is real.</note>, it is clear that Being itself is self-evident, so much so that whoever denies Being, denies himself. Indeed, by the very act of denying it, he posits that Being exists. 

Therefore, "Being"—or the <term>Word</term> <note>original: "verbum." This likely refers to the "Logos" or the divine creative principle through which all things were made.</note>—considered as inseparable from all things, is the proper subject of this Kabbalistic wisdom. Since this Being (or Word) is the first principle that regulates all things and is not regulated by anything else, it is obvious that this wisdom is the regulator of all other sciences by a long margin. Otherwise, among those things that regulate and those that are regulated, there would be an infinite regress <note>A logical dilemma where a series of causes or justifications goes on forever without a starting point; medieval scholars argued there must be a "first cause."</note>. 

And because every doctrine and discipline essentially comprises three things—namely, knowing the parts of its subject, knowing the desired goal, and knowing the means to reach that goal—this Kabbalistic wisdom is therefore divided into three parts:

The first part concerns the components of its subject, which are: 
1. **Goodness**
2. **Greatness**
3. **Duration**
4. **Power**
5. **Wisdom**
6. **Will**
7. **Virtue**
8. **Truth** 
9. **Glory** <note>These nine attributes are known in the Lullian tradition as the "Dignities." They are considered the fundamental building blocks of reality and the primary attributes of God.</note>

The desired **Goal** sought in this science is the attainment of the human intellect <note>The "perfection" or "fulfillment" of human understanding.</note>, but the **Means** to reach that goal is pure abstraction, which is:

<summary>This page defines "Being" as the core of all truth and the primary subject of "Kabbalistic wisdom" (the Lullian Art). It introduces the nine "Dignities"—fundamental attributes like Goodness and Power—that form the basis of this system of knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>Being, The Word, Kabbalistic wisdom, Infinite Regress, Dignities, Goodness, Greatness, Human Intellect, Abstraction</keywords>

...since the human intellect is an <term>abstract substance</term> <note>original: "substantia abstracta." In medieval scholastic philosophy, this refers to a reality—like the soul or an angel—that exists independently of physical matter.</note>, it is necessary that its object of understanding be abstract, and consequently, its act of understanding must be as well. Therefore, this work is divided into three treatises. In the first of these, the parts of the <term>proper subject</term> <note>original: "subiecti adæquati." This refers to the specific, fitting scope of this particular science.</note> will be explained, along with those things most attributed to it. In the second treatise, the sought-after goal is taught. In the third and last, the means to reach that very goal will be provided.

And because every work of method is established not only so that the human intellect may exercise itself, but so that it may serve as a remedy for forgetfulness, the first treatise comprises three parts. The first part consists of the **Alphabet** and the **figures** that are utilized in this work. The second concerns the <term>essences</term> <note>original: "quiditatum," literally "what-ness." It refers to the fundamental nature or defining characteristics of the subjects being discussed.</note> of the parts of this proper subject. The third concerns the **rules** which this science employs. All of these things wonderfully resist forgetfulness. 

This doctrine is called **Rabbala** <note>A variant spelling of Kabbalah used by the author.</note>, which according to the Hebrews is the same as the "reception of the truth of any thing divinely revealed to the rational soul." And according to modern Kabbalists, **Rabbala** is a name composed of two words, namely *abba* and *ala*. For *abba* in Arabic is the same as "father" in Latin <note>original: "pater."</note>, 

<summary>The author outlines the three-part structure of the book—covering the subject, the goal, and the means—and provides a mystical etymology for the term Kabbalah (Rabbala), defining it as divinely revealed truth.</summary>
<keywords>human intellect, abstract substance, memory, Kabbalah, Rabbala, Hebrew mysticism, Lullian figures, etymology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the etymological definition of the word "Kabbalah" (spelled "Kabbala" here) started on the previous page, specifically breaking down the components of the word through a mix of Arabic and Latin associations to link it to Christ and Divine Wisdom.</meta>

...tional soul. <note>This continues from the previous page's definition of "Kabbalah" as a "reception" (Hebrew: *qabbalah*) of truth.</note> And **ala** in Arabic is from where we get "my God" <note>original: "ala arabicé indé est quod deus meus." The author is likely associating the Arabic *Allah* with the Hebrew *Eli* (my God) to create a mystical etymology for the word Kabbalah.</note>; and since "my God" signifies nothing other than **Christ our blessed Lord**, who is truly the Son of God; and the "Son of God" signifies nothing other than **divine wisdom**. For this reason, we say that this word **Kabbalah**, which is written with the letter "k," signifies nothing else in Arabic, and in Latin nothing except **superabundant wisdom**. Therefore, *Kabba* is a <term>habit</term> <note>original: "habitus." In medieval philosophy, a 'habit' is a lasting disposition of the soul or mind that allows a person to perform certain actions—like reasoning or virtuous acts—with ease and consistency.</note> of the rational soul, capable of knowing divine things through right reason. Because of this, it is apparent that it deals with the greatest and even the divine; consequently, it ought to be called **divine science**.

->*The first part concerning the alphabet.*<-

Refers to things that are understood by the mind (like concepts or spiritual truths) rather than things perceived by the physical senses.</note> thing may be most easily united to the human intellect. Through these [letters and figures], the intellect may recognize itself as far-reaching and universal; for indeed, through a single letter of this alphabet, it comprehends many things to be known, from which it produces knowledge, which indeed...

<meta>This page continues the introduction to a mnemonic and philosophical system, likely the "Art" of Ramon Llull. It explains the "alphabet" used to categorize all knowledge. The previous page ended with "intelligible," and this page begins with the end of the word "indeed" (Latin: *quidem*), linking the two: "intelligible indeed."</meta>

...indeed <note>original: "dem," likely the conclusion of "quidem" (indeed) from the previous page.</note> the **alphabet** is most easily committed to memory, and for that reason it is very necessary in this science; also because without it, the **practitioner** <note>original: "opifex," literally an artisan or maker, here referring to the student or operator of this logical system.</note> of this method would be least able to exercise it. The **alphabet** is this, namely: **B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K**.

For **B** signifies **good** and its abstract quality <note>original: "abstractum." In this system, each letter represents both a concrete attribute (like "good") and its essence (like "goodness").</note>, **difference**, **God**, **justice**, **avarice**, and the question **"whether it is?"** **C** signifies **great** and its abstract quality **greatness**, **concordance**, **angel**, **prudence**, **gluttony**, and the question **"what is it?"** **D** signifies **enduring** and its abstract quality, **contrariety**, **heaven**, **fortitude**, **lust**, and the question **"from what is it?"** **E** signifies **powerful** and its abstract quality, **principle**, **man**, **temperance**, **pride**, and the question **"why is it?"** **F** signifies **wise** and its abstract quality, **the middle**, the **imaginative** <note>Referring to the faculty of the soul that deals with mental images.</note> power, **faith**, **sloth**, and the question **"how much is it?"** **G** signifies **willing** and its abstract quality, **the end**, the **sensitive** <note>Referring to the faculty of the soul related to sensory perception.</note> power, **hope**, **envy**, and the question **"of what kind is it?"** **H** signifies **virtuous** and its abstract quality, the **vegetative** <note>Referring to the faculty of the soul related to basic biological life and growth.</note> power, **majority**, **charity**, **wrath**, and the question **"when is it?"** **I** signifies **true** and its abstract quality, the **elemental** <note>Referring to the physical elements or basic principles of matter.</note> power, **equality**, **patience**, **falsehood**, and the question **"where is it?"** **K** signifies **glorious**, and its abstract quality, the **instrumen-** <note>The text cuts off mid-word; it likely continues as "instrumentum" (instrumentality or tool) on the following page.</note>

...power <note>original: "tatiuum," the conclusion of *vegetativum*, the vegetative power of the soul which governs growth and nutrition.</note>, **minority**, **piety**, **inconstancy**, and the questions **"how?"** and **"with what is it?"** Let these remarks suffice concerning the **alphabet** <term>alphabetum: the set of letters B through K used as shorthand for categories of reality</term>, therefore etc.

### Part Two: Concerning the Figures

The **figures** <note>In this system, figures are typically circular diagrams or "wheels" used to combine the concepts represented by the alphabet.</note> in this science were indeed invented and established according to the operations of the **intellect** <term>intellectus: the faculty of the mind that understands and reasons</term>. These operations are three: the **apprehension** <note>The initial mental grasping of a concept.</note> of all knowable concepts, the **division** and **composition** of those concepts, and **discourse** <note>original: "discursus," meaning the process of reasoning from one premise to another.</note> among them. There are four such figures.

The first of these is titled **A**. It is circular or spherical in shape, serving the **simple conversion** <note>In logic, "conversion" involves swapping the subject and predicate of a statement. A "simple" conversion means the statement remains true after the swap.</note> of all the first principles and rules of this **wisdom** <term>sapientia: here referring to the "Great Art," the systematic philosophical method being taught</term>, as is clearly apparent within it. This conversion presupposes the coupling of a **subject** and a **predicate** <note>The "subject" is what the sentence is about; the "predicate" is what is said about the subject.</note>. Examples of simple conversion are: **Being is good, and good is Being**; **Being is great, and great is Being**; **Being is eternal, and eternal is Being** <note>original: "esse est bonum, bonum est esse..." The author uses "esse" (to be) as a noun meaning "Being" or "Existence."</note>. The same must be said of the other parts of the subject suited to this wisdom. It is called **conversion** from the act of "turning around" <note>original: "a convertendo"</note>. For conversion is the transposition of the subject into the predicate, and vice versa; the species of which...

<summary>This page concludes the definition of the symbolic alphabet and introduces "Figure A," a circular diagram used for the logical "conversion" of primary principles like Being, Goodness, and Greatness.</summary>
<keywords>alphabet, Figure A, logic, simple conversion, Being, intellect, apprehension, subject and predicate, Ramon Llull, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of logical operations within the "Great Art." The previous page ended while defining the purpose of Figure A—the first of the system's circular diagrams—as a tool for "simple conversion." This page elaborates on the three specific types of conversion (reversing the subject and predicate of a statement) used to find truth.</meta>

...there are three, namely **simple**, **by accident**, and **by composition**. 

**Simple conversion** <note>original: "conuersio simplex." In formal logic, this is the process of swapping the subject and predicate of a statement without changing its truth value.</note> is the transposition of the subject into the predicate, and vice versa, while maintaining the same **quantity** <note>Whether the statement refers to "all" (universal) or "some" (particular) of a group.</note> and **quality** <note>Whether the statement is affirmative ("is") or negative ("is not").</note> as was stated in the examples above. 

However, **conversion by accident** <note>original: "conuersio per accidens."</note> is that in which the quantity is changed; for example, by saying: 
"Every **being** <term>ens: a fundamental philosophical term for anything that exists</term> is **good**, [therefore] some **good** is a **being**." 
"Every **being** is **great**, [therefore] some **great** thing is a **being**." 

In **conversion by composition** <note>original: "conuersionem per compositionem," often called contraposition. This involves using negative terms to rephrase a statement.</note>, there is a change of **finite terms** into **infinite terms** <note>In medieval logic, an "infinite term" is one defined by what it is not, such as "non-man" or "non-good," because it encompasses an "infinite" number of things that are not that specific thing.</note>, while the quantity and quality of the proposition remain the same. For example, by saying: 
"A **being** is **good**, [therefore] a **non-good** thing is a **non-being**." 
Or like this: "Every **non-being** is **non-good**, [therefore] every **good** thing is a **being**." 

And just as examples were given regarding "goodness" in every type of conversion, the same must be said regarding the other parts of the subject. 

Furthermore, in this **Figure A**, the human **intellect** <term>intellectus humanus: the capacity for rational thought</term> is meant to investigate the **communication** <note>The shared relationships or interconnections.</note> of all knowable beings. It also investigates the communication and properties of the subject and predicate of any proposition, and of each of them individually, so that through them the **essence** <term>quod quid est: literally "that which it is," referring to the fundamental nature or "quiddity" of a thing</term> might be discovered. This is possible because in this **Figure A**, there is something **most general** <note>original: "generalissimum." In the Lullian system, this refers to God or the "Divine Dignities" (Goodness, Greatness, etc.) which are the most universal categories of reality.</note> and it is it-

<summary>This page defines three methods of logical conversion—simple, by accident, and by composition—used to manipulate propositions within the system. It also explains that Figure A allows the human mind to find the essential nature of any "being" by examining how these concepts interconnect.</summary>

<keywords>logic, conversion, Figure A, being, goodness, quiddity, subject and predicate, infinite terms</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and logical investigation into the nature of existence, building on the previous discussion of "conversion" (swapping terms in a logical statement). It transitions from formal logic into metaphysics, specifically the hierarchy of reality found in Ramon Llull's "Great Art."</meta>

...[to be] **being** itself <note>original: "esse." In medieval philosophy, *esse* refers to the pure act of existing, whereas *ens* refers to a specific thing that exists.</note>, and some most specific thing, such as a **human**, an **angel**, or an **ox**, between which the **human intellect** <term>intellectus humanus: the capacity of the human mind to understand universal truths and categorize the world</term> has a scale of ascent and descent from the most general to the most specific. This is because under **"to be"** is contained **good**, and under **good** is contained **a being** <term>ens: a specific entity or thing that exists</term>, since every **being** is **good**, yet not every **good** is a **being**, as will be proven in the questions regarding **Figure A** <note>Figure A is the primary diagram in Llull's system, representing the divine attributes—like Goodness, Greatness, and Power—which he argues are the building blocks of all reality.</note>. Indeed, we will show in that same place that "to be"—which is the verb—is **good**, and yet it is not a **being**. 

For a **being** is, by its nature, posterior <note>meaning it comes "after" or is dependent upon</note> to **"to be"** and to **good** itself. For if **"to be"** itself were not **good** except through the existence of a **being**, it would follow that the **goodness** of the prior [the cause] would exist through the **goodness** of the posterior [the effect], which would be improper and absurd; because the **goodness** of the **first principle** <note>The "first principle" refers to God, the ultimate source from which all other things flow.</note> would be communicated from the **goodness** of that which is derived from the principle, and many other difficulties would follow from that position. 

Therefore, **"to be"** and **good** precede **being** itself, and a **being** is not **good** except from the **goodness** of **"to be"** itself, which is the **first principle** of all things; and **"to be"** is not **good** except through its very own essence, which communicates itself to every **being**, and so on. 

Under **being** itself, however, is **substance** <term>substantia: in this context, the underlying reality of a thing that supports its qualities</term>, under which is immediately **body** <note>original: "corpus," referring to physical or material existence.</note>. Under which is placed li- <note>The text cuts off, likely continuing to "living things" (viventia).</note>

<summary>This page outlines a metaphysical hierarchy where the pure act of "being" and "goodness" are primary, preceding the existence of specific entities. It explains that all things derive their goodness from these higher principles, descending from the general to the specific (substance, body, etc.).</summary>

<keywords>metaphysics, Figure A, Being vs. To Be, Goodness, First Principle, Hierarchy of Being, Substance, Scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ontological hierarchies, moving from the relationship between "being" and "goodness" into the specific categories of the "scale of being" used in Llull's logical system. It details how the mind classifies reality from the broadest category (Being) down to specific entities (Man and Angel).</meta>

...[contai]ning, under the living animal; under animal is placed the **rational**, and under the rational is placed the **human** or the **angel**. Therefore, it is clear that the **human intellect** <term>intellectus humanus: the capacity of the human mind to understand universal truths and categorize the world</term> possesses a scale for ascending from the most specific to the most general by gathering many things together, and for descending from the most general to the most specific by dividing many things through the use of contrary differences in the types of things. 

For example, **being** <note>original: "ens"</note> is divided into that which is **absolute** <note>original: "simpliciter," referring to being in its pure, unrestricted sense</note> and that which is **relative** <note>original: "secundum quod," meaning being in a certain respect or limited way</note>; or **good**, which is divided into the **created** and the **uncreated**. 

**Being** taken in its absolute sense is nothing other than **substance**, which is divided into the **corporeal** <note>physical or bodily</note> and the **incorporeal**. **Body** is then divided into the **living** and the **non-living**; and the **living** is divided into the **sensible** <term>sensibile: that which is capable of perception through the senses</term> and the **insensible**. The **sensible**—which is **animal**—is divided into the **rational** and the **irrational**. But the **rational** is further subdivided into the **discursive** and the **non-discursive**. The **discursive rational** being is **man** himself <note>In medieval philosophy, "discursive" reasoning refers to the human way of thinking step-by-step, moving from one premise to a conclusion</note>, whereas the **non-discursive rational** being is nothing other than an **angel** <note>Angels were thought to possess "intellection," an immediate, intuitive grasp of truth without needing to "reason through" a problem like humans do</note>.

The ascent in this figure is achieved by uniting the general type <note>original: "generis"</note> with its specific differences until one reaches the most general category. This figure was made **spherical** in this art <note>referring to the "Ars Magna" or the Great Art of Ramon Llull</note> for two reasons: first, so that it might be the most capable of holding all things that can be known; the second reason...

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Figure A in Ramon Llull's Art, specifically explaining its geometry and its representation of the divine attributes (the "dignities") as a system for the human mind to navigate abstract truths.</meta>

it is, so that it may better serve the approach and withdrawal <note>original: "accessui & recessui," referring to the movement of the mind toward a concept and back from it to understand its relations</note> which occur through the operation of the **intellect**. This figure, in its first division, is divided into three equal parts to give us to understand that whatever exists in **abstract substances** <term>substantiis abstractis: entities existing without physical form, such as the soul or angels</term>, and especially in the **divine essence** <note>original: "essentia diuina"</note>, exists according to a single equality. 

In the second division, however, it is divided into nine equal parts, to give us to understand that the meaning of any one part is **convertible** <note>meaning the terms are interchangeable or mutually predicated; for instance, God's goodness is also His greatness</note> with the meaning of any other part, as will be shown later in its proper place. The names of these nine parts are taken from the names of nine letters of the alphabet. For the first part is titled **B**, the second is titled **C**, the third **D**, the fourth **E**, the fifth **F**, the sixth **G**, the seventh **H**, the eighth **I**, and the ninth **K**. These parts are called **little spaces** <term>spaciola: the specific segments or cells within the circular diagram of the Art</term>, as is clearly visible in **Figure A** <note>The first of Llull's diagrams, representing nine attributes of God: Goodness, Greatness, Eternity, Power, Wisdom, Will, Virtue, Truth, and Glory</note>.

It is clear since in the category of **beginning** there are four types of **causes** <note>In Aristotelian thought, these are the material, formal, efficient, and final causes</note>, and the category of **substance**, and through **time** and **quantity** the nine other **predicaments** <term>predicamenta: also known as categories, these are the ten fundamental ways of describing any object—such as its quality, location, or action—as defined by Aristotle</term> which are distinct from the predicament of substance; wherefore it is evident that whatever is outside of these is nothing. 

In the angle of the **middle**, however, there are also three species: namely, the **middle of conjunction**, which is the middle between the **subject** and the **predicate** <note>This refers to the "middle term" in logic that connects two parts of a statement to reach a conclusion</note>. It is clear because a **human being** cannot be an **animal** except through the medium of **life**, nor a living being except through the medium of a **body**. Nor can it be a **body** except through the medium of **substance**, nor can it be a **substance** except through the medium of **being simply** <term>ens simpliciter: existence in its most basic form, before it is specified as a particular type of thing</term>. Nor can it be **being simply** except through the medium of the **good**, nor can it be **good** except through the medium of **being** <note>original: "esse," referring to the pure act of existing</note> itself. For all these things are prior to the human being; and just as has been said concerning the human being, so it must be said of other things in their own way through **ascent** <note>The logical process of moving from a specific creature up the chain of existence toward more universal concepts</note>.

between the **agent** and the **actable** <note>The "agibile" refers to that which is capable of being acted upon</note>; and similarly, **loving** is the middle between the **lover** and the **beloved**. The third species is the **middle of extremities**, such as a line, which is the middle between two points. This angle [the Middle] is a ladder of ascent and descent through all beings in their own way, because **essence** and **communications** are the means of extremities. This is because the essence of **goodness** is the middle between **greatness** and **duration** <note>In this philosophical system, "Greatness" and "Duration" (or Eternity) are fundamental attributes of God used to measure all things</note>, having within itself its own **bonifying** <term>bonificare: a technical term used here to describe the active process of "making good" or the outflowing of goodness</term> existing in the middle of the **bonifier** and the **bonifiable**. These are joined together in the act of bonifying, just as the lover and the beloved are joined in **loving**. These three—namely the lover, the beloved, and loving—are one undivided **lovability**, just as the bonifiable and the bonifying are one undivided **goodness**. And these three species are the ladder for ascending and descending to find the middle among all knowable beings. 

In like manner, we must also speak of the **angle of the end**. For the **end** is that state of being in which the **efficient cause** <note>The "efficient cause" is the agent or force that brings something into being</note> brings all beings to rest at their destination. This "end" contains three species within itself: namely, the **end of privation**, the **end of termination**, and the **end of causality**. An example of the first species is **death**, which is the privation of life; but

the **end of termination** <note>original: "finis terminationis"; this refers to a spatial or structural boundary rather than a goal or purpose</note> is like the border of a kingdom, or the points that are the end of a line’s termination, and the surface which is the end of a body’s termination. The **third triangle** is the triangle of **majority**, **equality**, and **minority**, which is also universal to all knowable beings in its own way. This is clear because everything that exists is either in the genus of majority, or in the genus of equality, or in the genus of minority, and nothing exists outside of these three genera. This is evident because it is impossible to identify any being that is not contained under one of these three genera: for under the genus of majority is **substance**, and under the genus of minority is **accident** <note>In this philosophical context, "substance" is the primary reality of a thing, while "accidents" are secondary qualities like color or size that depend on the substance to exist</note>. From this, it clearly appears that whatever exists is either substance or accident, and outside of these there is nothing.

Of the **first species** is the substance of the heavens, which is greater than the substance of fire, and the substance of man, which is greater in goodness than the substance of an elephant.

An example of the **third species** is substance itself, which is greater than an accident. The **angle of equality** contains three species within itself: namely, the equality of substances, the equality of accidents, and the equality of substances and accidents. 

An example of the **first species** is the equality of two individuals of the human species—for instance, **Socrates** and **Plato**—who are substances equal in **humanity** <note>original: "homeitate"; a technical term for the essence of being human</note> and rationality. 

But an example of the **second species** is the equality between understanding and willing, or loving.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of Figure T within Ramon Llull’s "Art." Having previously established the color-coding for the nine principles (Difference, Concordance, Contrariety; Beginning, Middle, End; and Majority, Equality, Minority), the author now explains the specific logical and metaphysical functions of the first "green" triangle—comprising Difference, Concordance, and Contrariety—and how these categories govern both the physical world and human reasoning.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<sig>B ij</sig>

...it encompasses nine categories of knowable things <note>original: "genera scibilium nouem"</note>, each of which contains all existing beings, although the category of **Difference** is more general than the other categories. This is because **Difference** is predicated of more things than **Concordance** and **Contrariety**. This is evident, since Socrates and Plato are distinguished in reality <note>original: "distinguitur realiter"</note> and yet they are the same in species—that is, formally. Similarly, God and an angel are distinguished in reality and formally, and yet in them there is no **Contrariety** <note>In Llull's system, Contrariety only exists between things that can oppose or destroy one another; divine and celestial beings are distinct but not in opposition</note>. In the same way, the stars are distinguished, and yet in them there is no **Contrariety**.

Furthermore, **Difference** is the cause of plurality because it separates and distinguishes; but **Concordance** is the cause of unity, because it both places and unites many things into a single being. But **Contrariety** corrupts and dissolves. For this reason, Democritus <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher known for his atomic theory; here he is cited for the idea that "strife" and "friendship" govern the universe</note> says: "Strife among beings comes from contrariety, and friendship among them is concordance" <note>original: "Lis in entibus est à contrarietate, & amicitia in eis est concordantia"</note>.

Third, it should be noted that just as the green triangle <note>Referring to the first set of three principles in Figure T, which Llull colored green for mnemonic purposes</note> exists naturally in a subject, so the human intellect is "discursive" <note>meaning it moves step-by-step through reasoning</note> in a moral sense. It functions by distinguishing, bringing into harmony, assenting, or dissenting through all its species—namely, those of **Difference**, **Concordance**, and **Contrariety**.

<summary>This page explains why Difference is the most universal category of Figure T, noting that things can be different (like stars or angels) without being in conflict (Contrariety). It defines Difference as the root of plurality and Concordance as the root of unity, comparing these to the ancient philosophical concepts of Strife and Friendship.</summary>

And since **Difference** is objectively more general than the others, as has been shown by the examples of the categories <note>original: "generibus"</note>, it follows that when the human intellect draws itself down from the first step of the ladder <note>Referring to the "Ladder of Being" (Scala Naturae), a hierarchy from God down to inanimate matter</note> to the level of the senses: then it is not entirely general, nor entirely specific; but when it descends to the individual: then it is simply specific.

The first example is when a **Difference** is stated to exist between one intellectual being and another intellectual being, such as between God and an angel.

The second example is when a **Difference** is stated between the intellectual and the sensual, such as between the soul and the body, or between substance and accident <note>In scholastic philosophy, a "substance" is a thing that exists by itself (like a tree), while an "accident" is a quality that exists only in something else (like the greenness of the tree's leaves)</note>.

The third example is when a **Difference** is stated between one sensual thing and another sensual thing, such as between a stone and wood, or between one accident and another accident. 

When a **Difference** is said to exist between Plato and Cicero <note>A standard philosophical pairing of individuals to illustrate "particular" or "individual" differences</note>: then it is particular. Therefore, etc.

And these examples ought to be applied in their own way to all the other triangles <note>Referring to the other groupings in Llull’s Art, such as the triangles for Beginning/Middle/End or Majority/Equality/Minority</note>. 

Finally, it must be noted that this **Figure T** is the instrument of the human intellect with which it acts upon **Figure A** <note>Figure A represents the circle of the sixteen Divine Attributes or "Dignities," such as Goodness, Greatness, and Power. Figure T provides the relational logic—Difference, Concordance, etc.—to analyze them</note>, by placing a **Difference** between the good and the great, between the good and the eternal, between the good and the powerful: and so on for the rest of the meanings.

## Discourse on the Third Figure.

<image-desc>Decorative floral drop cap initial 'T' inside a square frame.</image-desc>
**T**he **Third Figure** is indeed composed of the first two—namely, **Figure A** <note>representing the nine Divine Dignities: Goodness, Greatness, Eternity, etc.</note> and **Figure T** <note>representing relational principles like Difference, Accordance, and Contrariety</note>—to signify that whatever is implied in them is also implied in this third one. For the letter **B** that is in the first figure is equivalent to the **B** of this third figure; and similarly, the **B** of the second figure is equivalent to the **B** of the third. As has been said of B, so it must be said of the other letters of the Alphabet <note>The Llullian Alphabet uses letters B through K to represent specific philosophical concepts</note> of this teaching. It must be noted that this figure is constructed from thirty-six quadrangular shapes <note>referring to the cells in a grid or table used for combining terms</note> as is clearly visible in it, each of which [contains] many things in itself

The purpose for which this figure contains **thirty-six square figures** is so that the human intellect, in all parts of **being** <note>original: "esse"; existence itself</note> itself, recognizes that it can form many questions and deduce many reasons from those very parts of being. For example:

*   Goodness is powerful
*   Goodness is knowable
*   Goodness is lovable
*   Goodness is virtuous
*   Goodness is true
*   Goodness is glorious

Therefore, etc. 

And it should be noted that the condition of this **third figure** <note>The Third Figure is a grid or table used to cross-reference two concepts</note> is that one of its squares is in accordance with another; for instance, the square of **B. C.** <note>Goodness and Greatness</note> and so on for others; by a similar condition, they accord with one another to generate **wisdom** in the **human intellect** <note>original: "intellectu humano"</note>. Therefore, etc. 

Furthermore, it should be noted that this figure serves the **second operation of the intellect** <note>In traditional logic, the "first operation" is simply understanding a concept. The "second operation" is forming a judgment—linking two concepts together to make a statement like "The sky is blue"</note>, whose proper function is to **compose** and to **divide** <note>original: "componere, & diuidere"; "Composing" means affirming a statement (X is Y), while "dividing" means denying it (X is not Y)</note>. 
*   An example of the first (composing): **"Every good thing is just."**
*   An example of the second (dividing): **"No good thing is greedy."** 

And let these things suffice regarding the third figure.

->**Discourse on the fourth figure.**<-

The **fourth figure** <note>The Fourth Figure is Llull’s most famous invention: a series of concentric, moving paper wheels called a "volvelle." By rotating the wheels, the user can generate every possible combination of the alphabet’s principles</note> is composed of **three circles**, of which the largest is **immobile**; the remaining two are **mobile**, as clearly appears in the figure itself.

<summary>This page concludes the explanation of the Third Figure as a tool for logical judgment and introduces the Fourth Figure, a device of concentric moving circles used to generate all possible conceptual combinations.</summary>

...[ter]ms signifying divine being, we say that **being** is more common than all others and is self-evident; it does not require explanation, nevertheless, its parts are explained here. Hence, the **Good** considered in this matter is that **being** for the sake of which all things are good; therefore, it does not belong to the **Good** except to act as good. For this reason, **good-making being** <term>good-making being</term> <note>original: "esse bonificans"; in this system, the divine attributes are seen as active powers that communicate their nature to the rest of creation</note> is nothing other than the **Good** itself.

The **Great** is that **being** for the sake of which all things are great. It does not belong to the **Great**, therefore, except to act as great. Thus, **great-making being** is great, and for that reason, nothing belongs to it except to make great.

The **Enduring** is that **being** for the sake of which all things have duration; hence, **duration-making being** <term>duration-making</term> <note>original: "durificans"</note> is enduring and eternal. Therefore, nothing belongs to the **Eternal** except to make eternal <note>original: "æternificare"</note>, whether according to individualized being or according to specific being <note>The author distinguishes between the eternity of an individual soul or entity and the eternity of a whole species or class of things</note>.

The **Powerful** is that **being** for the sake of which all things are powerful; therefore, there is **power-making being**, whose proper function is nothing but to empower.

The **Wise** is that **being** for the sake of which all things are knowable; hence, **wisdom-making being** is nothing but the **Wise**, whose proper function is nothing but to make wise <note>original: "sapientificare"</note>.

The **Willing** is that **being** for the sake of which all things have will; therefore, **will-making being** <note>original: "voluptuosificans"; while this word often relates to pleasure, in this context of "the Willing" (volens), it refers to the active exercise of the will</note> is nothing but the **Willing**.

[Greatness] is the act and perfection <note>original: "profectio" in OCR, likely a transcription of "perfectio" (perfection) to match the repetitive formula on this page</note> of the great, by reason of which that which is great acts only as something great; from which goodness and duration, through that very greatness, are made great, encompassing all extremities and penetrating through the whole of creation. 

**Duration**, or permanence, is the act and perfection of the permanent, by reason of which that which is permanent acts only as something permanent. 

**Power**, or power-ness <term>power-ness</term> <note>original: "potentietas"; a technical term for the abstract essence of power</note>, is the act and perfection of the powerful, by reason of which that which is powerful acts only as something powerful; and through it, all things act and react upon one another, one resisting the other. 

**Wisdom**, or wisdom-ness <term>wisdom-ness</term> <note>original: "sapientieitas"; the abstract quality that allows a wise being to function</note>, is the act and perfection of the wise, by reason of which that which is wise acts only as something wise; and through it, all things are knowable. 

**Will** is the act and perfection of the willing, by reason of which that which wills acts only as something willing; and through this will, all things are lovable. 

**Virtue** is the act and perfection of the virtuous, by reason of which that which is virtuous acts only as something virtuous. 

**Truth** is the act and perfection of the true, by reason of which that which is true acts only as something true; and through it, goodness, greatness, eternity <note>original: "æternitas"; here functioning as the perfection of duration</note>, surpassing wisdom, will, and virtue are made into objects of the intellect <note>the faculty of the mind that understands and reasons</note>, together with glory itself.

### *On the necessary rules, the fourth part of the first treatise.*

Moreover, the rules of this work that are most necessary are ten in number: namely, *whether*, *what*, *of what*, *why*, *how much*, *of what kind*, *when*, *where*, *how*, and *with what*. The first, which is *whether* (or *if*), is attributed to **B**; *what* is attributed to **C**; *of what* is attributed to **D**; *why* is attributed to **E**; *how much* is attributed to **F**; and *of what kind* is attributed to... <note>In the Lullian Art, these ten questions (the "Rules") are assigned letters on a wheel to allow the practitioner to systematically investigate any subject by rotating the wheel.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the definitions of Lull's Relative Principles (Majority, Equality, and Minority) and introduces the Ten Rules—a systematic set of questions used to analyze any subject.</summary>
<keywords>Majority, Equality, Minority, Kabbalah, Quiddity, Lullian Rules, Logic, Scholasticism</keywords>

We pick up mid-sentence with the assignment of Rule G.</meta>

...is attributed to **G**; and *when* <note>original: "quando"</note> is attributed to **H**; and *where* <note>original: "ubi"</note> is attributed to **I**; and *how* and *with what* <note>original: "quomodo & cum quo"</note> are attributed to **K**. The species <term>species</term> <note>In Lullian logic, "species" refers to the specific sub-questions or categories that fall under a general rule.</note> of these rules number fifty in total.

For **B** contains three species under itself: namely, *doubt*, *affirmation*, and *negation*. And the letter **C** <note>The rule for "What" (Quid)</note> contains *what it is*, *what it has in itself*, *what it is in another*, and *what it has with another*; thus, there are four species of **C**.

But **D** <note>The rule for "Of what" (De quo)</note> contains three species under itself: namely, *from what*, *of what*, and *to whom it belongs*. The letter **E** <note>The rule for "Why" (Propter quid)</note> contains two species under itself: namely, *formally* <note>original: "formaliter"; relating to the internal essence or "form" of a thing</note> and *finally* <note>original: "finaliter"; relating to the purpose or "end" of a thing</note>. But the letter **F** <note>The rule for "How much" (Quantum)</note> contains another two species under itself: *continuously* <note>original: "continuative"; referring to unbroken extension like time or a line</note> and *discretely* <note>original: "discretive"; referring to distinct units like numbers</note>.

The letter **G** <note>The rule for "Of what kind" (Quale)</note> also comprises two species under itself: namely, *what kind of essence* <note>original: "quale quid"</note> and *what kind of shared quality* <note>original: "quale communicatum"</note>, or *properly* and *appropriately*.

But the letter **H** <note>The rule for "When" (Quando)</note> comprises fifteen species under itself: namely, the four species of letter **C**, the three species of letter **D**, and the eight species of letter **K**. This was done because the essence of time <note>original: "essentia temporis"</note> is very difficult to understand; therefore, rule **H** must be applied to the species of rules **C**, **D**, and **K**.

Similarly, the rule of letter **I** <note>The rule for "Where" (Ubi)</note> contains under itself all the species of rules **C**, **D**, and **K**. And the rule of letter **R** <note>Likely a scribal error for "K," the final rule dealing with "How" and "With what," as Rule R does not exist in the standard Lullian alphabet.</note> [comprises] two... <meta>catchword: com-</meta>

<summary>This page details the fifty "species" or sub-categories of the ten Lullian Rules, explaining how complex questions like Time (H) and Space (I) are analyzed by combining other rules.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the breakdown of the species of the ten Rules (B through K) in Ramon Llull's logical system. The previous page ended while introducing the species of Rule G (Of what kind). We now move to the final rules and the philosophical basis for doubt and understanding.</meta>

...namely, the genus of **modality** <term>modalitas</term> <note>This refers to the "manner" or "how" of a thing, corresponding to Rule K.</note> of the rule, and the genus of **instrumentality** <term>instrumentalitas</term> <note>This refers to the "means" or "with what" a thing acts, also part of Rule K.</note>, as will be shown in its examples, why, etc. 

And since indeed regarding **nothingness** <note>original: "nihil"</note> and **simple being** <note>original: "simpliciter esse"; refers to existence in its most basic, self-evident form</note> no doubt arises—for nothingness causes no wonder in us, and simple being is manifest in itself—therefore it requires no demonstration, nor does it contain any doubt within itself. For there is no one of sound mind who can deny being itself, as was proven in the preface of this work. 

Consequently, we say that all doubts can only occur in the middle ground between these two: namely, simple being and nothingness. Through this "whether" <note>original: "utrum"; the fundamental question of existence or possibility</note> they possess doubt, affirmation, and possible negation; this exists in a state of balance, assuming that the intellect is not bound to believe it. This "believing" is nothing other than an internal act of the intellect itself, just as "understanding" is. Therefore, the intellect conceives only that part of the doubt through which it can understand, by assuming that truth is being predicated of it.

<summary>This page concludes the categorization of the logical "Rules" by explaining that Rule K covers modality and instrumentality. It further argues that logic and doubt exist only in the middle ground between the self-evident certainties of "Being" and "Nothing."</summary>
<keywords>modality, instrumentality, Rule K, Being, Nothingness, doubt, intellect, belief, Ramon Llull</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of the "Rules" (the fundamental questions) used in Ramon Llull’s "Great Art." Having previously discussed the general nature of doubt and simple being, the text now focuses on Rule C (the question "What?").</meta>

**Rule C** <note>original: "regula C."; this refers to the second of Llull's nine questions, "Quid?" or "What?"</note> contains, first, the **quidditative definition** <term>quiditatiuum</term> <note>A definition that explains the "quiddity" or the "what-ness" (the essential nature) of a thing.</note> of a thing. For example, if it is asked: "What is **being**?" <note>original: "ens"</note>, it must be answered that it is the "first created thing." Or, if it is asked: "What is the **human intellect**?", it must be answered that it is the "power of the soul whose proper function is to understand."

The second species of **Rule T** <note>While the text mentions "Rule T," it is here being used to categorize a sub-type of Rule C ("What"). In Llull’s system, Rule T often refers to the principles of Relation, such as Difference and Concordance, used to further define a subject.</note> is: "What does a thing have in itself?" For example, if it were asked: "What does **being** have in itself?", one should answer that it possesses the **power to be** <note>original: "entitatiuum"</note>, **that which is being** <note>original: "entitatum"</note>, and the **act of being** <note>original: "entitare"</note>. 

Similarly, if it were asked of the **intellect** what it has in itself, one must answer that it possesses the **intellectual power** <note>original: "intellectiuum"</note>, **that which is understood** <note>original: "intellectum"</note>, and the **act of understanding** <note>original: "intelligere"</note>. If one were to ask of the intellect: "What would it be without these components? Would it be **idle** <note>original: "ociosus"; meaning purposeless or non-functioning</note> in nature?", the answer must be: "Yes."

The third species of **Rule C** is: "What is it in another?" For example, by saying: "Is the intellect in another?", one must answer that it is a "good understanding being" within **Goodness**, or within **Greatness** a "great understanding being." In **Duration** <note>original: "duratione"</note> it is enduring; in **Power** <note>original: "potestate"</note> it is powerful; in **Wisdom** <note>original: "sapientia"</note> it is wise; in **Will** <note>original: "voluntate"</note> it is willing; in **Virtue** <note>original: "virtute"</note> it is virtuous; in **Truth** <note>original: "veritate"</note> it is true; and in **Glory** <note>original: "gloria"</note> it is glorious. 

And just as this example has been given regarding the intellect itself through these four species...

<summary>This page defines "Rule C" (the question "What?") in Ramon Llull's system, explaining how to define a thing's essence, its internal components, and its relationship to universal virtues like Goodness and Truth.</summary>

For this reason, time, insofar as it is primitive, is the **first being in its genus**, under which are contained years, months, days, and hours, and...

*   The first is when it is asked: **how is the intellect a part?** 
*   The second is when it is asked: **how is it a part within a part?** 
*   The third rule is when it is asked of the intellect, or any other being: **how is it a part within its whole?**
*   The fourth and final species is when it is asked of the intellect, or any other whole being: **how is it a whole within its parts**, and **how does it transmit its likeness outside of itself?**

...through that movement by which it is led through **intelligible species** <note>original: "species intelligibiles"; these are the mental forms or universal concepts that the mind uses to recognize and understand objects.</note>, and through that movement by which it is able to find the very **essence** <term>essence (quod quid est): literally "the what it is," referring to the core definition or quiddity of a thing.</term> of a thing. The **letter K** <note>In the Lullian system, Rule K represents the questions "How?" (Quomodo) and "With what?" (Cum quo).</note> is the genus of **instrumentality** <note>original: "instrumentalitas"; the study of the tools or means by which an effect is produced.</note>, containing four species beneath it. 

The first species occurs when one asks, for example, about the **informative power** <term>informative power (virtus informativa): the biological or metaphysical force believed to organize and shape a living being during its development.</term>: "With what does it act?" 

To this, one must answer that it acts with its own **power of forming** <note>original: "informalitas"; here referring to the capacity of a form to shape matter.</note>, organizing it as is required in **signate matter** <term>signate matter (materia signata): matter that has been given specific dimensions and individual characteristics.</term> through **celestial heat** <note>The vitalizing warmth believed to come from the stars and heavens.</note>, joined to the **natural heat of the designated seed**. 

Alternatively, one might ask about the **intellect**: "With what does it perform its acts of understanding?" 

To this, the answer is that it understands through the application of one **foreign species** <note>An "alien" or "foreign" species refers to an abstract concept that has been "imported" into the mind from the outside world.</note> to another, placing them within its own proper intelligible nature—just as light places colors into a mirror. This is part of its essence, and this is called the **receiving intellect** <note>original: "intellectus recipiens"; the aspect of the mind that takes in and holds information.</note>. 

But it is called **particular** when, using only one specific image among many, it understands some individual being through practice or by committing it to memory.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Rule K (Instrumentality) within the Lullian system. It moves from the general definition of how the intellect perceives external things to a categorization of "instruments"—the means by which actions are performed—ranging from physical organs and tools to abstract logical arguments.</meta>

The **intellect** <note>The source text uses "lectus," a common abbreviation for *intellectus*.</note> transmits the likeness of things outside of itself; to this, one must answer that it does this with its own **intellective** <term>intellectivum: the active power of the mind that performs the act of thinking.</term>, **intelligible** <term>intelligibile: the object or concept being thought about.</term>, and **act of understanding** <term>intelligere: the actual process or verb of thinking.</term>. With these, it causes foreign **intelligible species** <term>species: mental images or representations that represent external objects within the mind.</term> to be understood, and through **memory** to be remembered, and through the **will** to be loved, or chosen to be pursued or avoided. 

And by this rule <note>Rule K: The Rule of Instrumentality, asking "With what?" (Cum quo?).</note> inquiry is made regarding all instruments of things natural and artificial, both in the genus of **spirituality** and in the genus of **corporeality** <note>original: "corporalitas"; the state of having a physical body or material existence.</note>, as well as in the genus of abstract things and in the genus of concrete things. For the rule of instrumentality contains within itself all categories of instruments. 

First, in the genus of concrete things (or corporeality), it contains the organic members of the human body and the primary qualities of the **elements** <note>The four elements: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water, which were thought to be the "instruments" of natural change.</note> with which they act and react upon one another. This pertains to the instruments of natural things. 

As for **artificial instruments**, the rule contains all the tools of craftsmen, such as the blacksmith’s **hammer** and **anvil**. Moreover, it contains in the genus of **spirituality** all spiritual instruments and **argumentations** <note>original: "argumentationes"; logical proofs or syllogisms used by the mind to reach a conclusion.</note>,

The **major propositions** <note>the first statement in a three-part syllogism</note> of these two syllogisms are self-evident. Therefore, a good thing is great because it is good through goodness (which is the act and perfection of the good), by reason of which a good thing acts only in a good way. Therefore, the good is great.

### On the multiplication of entities through the fourth figure.

For **entities** <term>entia: the basic principles or "beings" of the system, such as Goodness and Greatness</term> are multiplied through the small spaces of the **Fourth Figure** <note>a famous Lullian device consisting of three concentric, overlapping circles of different sizes</note>, up to the number 252. This is done by rotating the circle of the middle disk under the smaller and immobile circle. For example, let the letter **C** of the middle disk be placed under letter **B** of the larger circle (which remains still), and **D** of the smaller circle under **C** of the middle one; then there will be formed in the mind one **chamber** <term>camera: a "cell" or logical compartment formed by the intersection of three letters</term> or space **b. c. d.** 

This is the first space of the first column in the **table** <term>tabula: a reference grid where the results of the rotating wheels are recorded</term>, while **C. D. E.** is the first space of the second column in the table, and **d. e. f.** is the first space of the third column in the table. This continues through the rest of the first spaces of the columns in the table up to the seventh number, which ends in the space **h. i. k.** Through this first revolution of the disks, the mind perceives the communication between the **subject and predicate** <term>subiectum & prædicatum: the two parts of a logical statement—the thing being discussed and what is being said about it</term> of any **proposi-** <note>The word is cut off: "propositio" (proposition)</note>

...which is; for through that which is **goodness**, the first **reason** <note>original: "ratio"; here meaning the essential principle, defining logic, or account of a thing.</note> of goodness—or its **quiddity** <term>quiditatis: "quiddity" or the "what-ness" of a thing; its essential nature.</term>—is obtained. Through that which is **great**, a second reason is applied to it, which is the act of the **great good**; by reason of which the great good acts as a great good. Similarly, the reason is tripled for the good itself when it is called **great and eternal goodness**. This is evident because there is a tripled act within goodness itself, by reason of which the good acts as something good, great, and eternal. 

Through **power**, goodness itself acquires a quadrupled act, through which it attains the state of being good, great, eternal, and powerful. By reason of this, the great, eternal, and powerful good acts only in a manner similar to itself. 

Just as we have given an example of these four quadrupled reasons applied to goodness itself (or to the "good" itself), so must an example be given for all other parts of the subject of this **wisdom**. In this method, these parts are called **principles**. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the "first combination" from the previous page, specifically how the primary principles of the Lullian Art—known as Dignities—interact. It moves from the fourfold multiplication of Goodness to even higher levels of complexity, while defining the logical boundaries between positive and negative principles.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>iiij</sig>

**Contrariety** and **minority** can be combined with all private <note>original: "privatis"; in this logical context, it refers to "privative" terms—those that indicate a lack or a negative state, like darkness being the absence of light.</note> terms, just as **goodness** can be combined with all positive terms.

And for that reason, **goodness** is quintupled <note>multiplied fivefold</note> through **wisdom**; and it is sextupled <note>multiplied sixfold</note> through **will**; and it is multiplied sevenfold through **virtue**; and it is multiplied eightfold through **truth**; and through **glory** its reason <note>original: "ratio"; here meaning the essential logic, formal principle, or defining account of the thing.</note> is multiplied ninefold. Through this, it becomes the **act** of that which is good, great, eternal, powerful, wise, willing, virtuous, true, and glorious. By reason of this, the good acts in a manner similar to itself, and brings [its results] forth. 

Furthermore, through **difference**, it is multiplied tenfold; and through **concordance** <note>original: "concordantiam"; the principle of harmony or agreement between different things.</note> it is multiplied elevenfold. This is because "concordant goodness" is the act of the good, by reason of which a concordant good acts as a concordant good. 

And because **goodness** is the opposite and the contrary of **malice**, and all **concordance** is the opposite of **contrariety**, it follows that goodness cannot be combined with contrariety—just as we stated above. This is because whatever is opposed to concordance is also opposed to goodness; but since contrariety is opposed to concordance, it is therefore opposed to goodness.

And if anyone were to argue in this way: "Every cause of **generation** <term>generationis: "generation"; the philosophical term for the process of something being created, born, or coming into existence.</term> is good, but contrariety is a cause [of generation]..."

<summary>This page describes how the principle of Goodness multiplies its power and "reason" as it combines with other virtues like Wisdom, Truth, and Concordance. It also establishes a logical rule: because Goodness relies on Harmony (Concordance), it cannot be combined with Conflict (Contrariety).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical exploration of how "goodness" (one of the primary Lullian Dignities) can or cannot be combined with other principles. It specifically addresses the problem of "contrariety" (opposition), which was left off at the end of the previous page.</meta>

...to generation <note>Continuing from the previous page: "whatever is opposed to generation."</note>. Therefore, if it is asked whether **contrariety** is good, it is answered that the contrariety of the elements is a cause of generation by accident <note>original: *per accidens*; occurring as a side effect or secondary result rather than by the primary nature of a thing.</note>. Therefore, it is only good by accident. However, since contrariety is the cause of corruption by its very nature <note>original: *per se*</note>, and corruption is evil, it follows that contrariety is evil by its very nature.

But if it is good, it is good through something else <note>original: *per aliud*</note>. Furthermore, **goodness** is combined with the **beginning**, the **middle**, and the **end**, and with **minority** and **equality**—though with minority not at all, except in a comparative sense. Because of this, it must be said that it is combined twelvefold, by saying: "goodness is a beginning-giver" <note>original: *bonitas est principians*; in Lullian logic, this refers to goodness acting as the source or starting point of an action.</note>.

And it is also combined thirteenfold, fourteenfold, fifteenfold, and sixteenfold with the other principles of the second **Figure T** <note>A symbolic diagram in Llull's system representing relational concepts such as Beginning, Middle, End, Equality, and Difference.</note>. And it should be noted that just as it was said concerning the combination of **goodness** with all principles in the examples given, so too must each of the other principles of the subject be combined.

For example, by saying: **greatness** <note>original: *magnitudo*</note> is good, greatness is eternal, greatness is powerful, greatness is wise, greatness is willing, greatness is virtuous, greatness is true, and greatness is glorious. Moreover, the utility that is acquired from the combination...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Lullian system of logic, moving from the combination of principles to the application of specific "Rules." ...of the principles and rules is that the **human intellect** <note>original: *intellectus humanus*</note> in its process of knowing should apprehend that their generality is absolute. Through this, the intellect is made more cautious against those who wish to tear apart the truth.

Therefore, beginning from the combination of **goodness** with the rules, it is first asked: **Whether**, according to **Rule B** <note>In the Lullian Art, Rule B corresponds to the question "Whether a thing exists" (*Utrum*).</note>, goodness is the **first principle** in the genus of goodness? To which it must be responded that it is; otherwise, nothing would be good. For it is evident that in any genus in which one cannot grant a first, one cannot grant a last, and consequently not a middle either. Therefore, nothing would be good, which is absurd and highly inconvenient. This has been examined through the first species of Rule B.

But through the first species of **Rule C** <note>Rule C corresponds to the question "What is it?" (*Quid*). Llull uses these rules to define the essence and properties of a subject.</note>, it is asked: **What is general goodness?** To which it must be responded that it is the act of a general good infusing its goodness into all substances. This is because goodness in **matter** <note>original: *materia*; the underlying "stuff" of the universe which has the potential to become something.</note> is **pure potentiality** <note>original: *pura potentia*; the capacity to receive a form but not yet having any specific characteristics.</note>, and in **form** <note>original: *forma*; the organizing principle that gives matter its specific identity and reality.</note> it is an act sustained in matter. Wherefore, both matter and form are sustained by goodness itself.

And if it is asked through the second species of Rule C: **What does general goodness have in itself?** To which it must be responded...

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Lullian "Art," a system of logic designed by Ramon Llull. ...it is, because it has its general **correlatives** <note>In Lullian logic, "correlatives" are the three components of any principle: the active power, the object it acts upon, and the act itself (e.g., the power to do good, that which receives good, and the act of doing good).</note>, without which it cannot be a **general principle** <note>original: *generale principium*</note>. (*Quare*).</note>, as when it is asked: **From what does general goodness come?** To which it must be answered that it is from itself, and is derived from no other. <meta>This page continues the discussion of God’s attributes (the Principles), moving from the identification of these attributes with God's essence to how these same attributes manifest differently across the hierarchy of being—from God to angels, to the celestial bodies, and finally to humans.</meta>

**God:** <note>original: *deus*</note> And it should be noted, secondly, that these **Principles** <note>The fundamental attributes of Ramon Llull's "Art," such as Goodness, Greatness, and Power, which he uses to explain all of reality.</note> have one condition in God, and another in an angel, and one in the heavens, and another in man, and so on for others in their own way. This is because the **essential nature** <note>original: *ratio essentiæ*; the logical definition or fundamental character of a thing's being.</note> of the divine essence is different from the nature of the angelic essence. The reason is that in the divine essence, **goodness** <note>original: *bonitas*</note> is infinite; for this reason, God cannot merely be called "good," but rather "of infinite goodness." This is not the case in the angelic essence, because the goodness of its essence is a finite and dependent goodness, whereas the divine goodness—which is infinite—is dependent on nothing. Otherwise, it would not be the goodness of God. 

Similarly, however, the goodness of the essence of the **heavens** <note>original: *cœli*; referring to the physical celestial spheres which, in medieval cosmology, were thought to be made of a different material than Earthly things.</note> is distinct from the goodness of the angelic essence, because in the heavens it is **corporeal** <note>original: *corporea*; having a physical body or substance.</note>, while in an angel it is **incorporeal**. They are likewise distinguished by this: the goodness of an angel exists alongside **eternity**, while the goodness of the heavens exists alongside **time**. There is also another difference: angelic goodness is a goodness that understands and moves, whereas the goodness of the heavens is understood and moved—at least in regard to its **location** <note>original: *ad vbi*; literally "to the 'where'," referring to spatial motion through the sky.</note>. <meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how the "Principles" (like Goodness) are applied to different "Subjects" or levels of reality. ...the goodness of the **sensitive** <note>original: *sensitiui*; referring to the animal kingdom, characterized by the ability to feel.</note> is distinguished from the goodness of the **vegetative** <note>original: *vegetatiui*; referring to plants</note> by its nature as a **tactile** being <note>original: *esse tactiuum*; the ability to perceive through touch, which distinguishes animals from plants.</note>, and the goodness of the vegetative is distinguished from the goodness of the **elemental** <note>original: *elementatiui*; referring to non-living matter composed of the four elements.</note> by its nature as a **nutritive** being. 

Furthermore, the goodness of the elemental differs from the goodness of the **instrumental** <note>original: *instrumentatiui*; in this system, this refers to the powers or "instruments" of the soul or mechanical movement.</note> by being a **mixture** <note>original: *mixtum*; a composite material substance.</note>; specifically, the goodness of the instrumental exists through goodness and the nature of being both the **mover** and the **moved**. The **goodness of God** is His act and His perfection, so that He may perform an **incomprehensible primary good** that precedes eternity. But **angelic goodness** is the act and perfection of an angel, so that it may perform a **comprehensible good** that exists simultaneously with eternity. The **goodness of the heavens** is its act, by reason of which the heavens produce a **temporal good**, or perpetual time. But the **goodness of man** is an act, by reason of which man performs an **intelligible temporal good** <note>A good that can be understood by the intellect but exists within the flow of time.</note>. 

And just as examples have been given for these four subjects, it can be exemplified for the other five subjects in their own way.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Nine Subjects" from the previous page. Having touched upon lower beings, it now addresses the "fourth condition" of the system: the requirement that the highest subjects (starting with God) must be defined by the most noble and infinite principles.</meta>

The **fourth condition** is that according to the **nobility** <note>original: *nobilitatem*; referring to the ontological rank or spiritual excellence of a being.</note> and height of the **subjects**, more noble and higher **principles** <note>original: *principia*; the fundamental attributes such as Goodness, Greatness, and Power that Llull uses to describe reality.</note> must be attributed to them. For example, God is more noble and higher than all others, and therefore more noble and higher principles are owed to Him, and so on.

And although God can be understood through all principles and rules—by which, or from which, God is of **infinite goodness**, infinite greatness, infinite duration, infinite power, infinite wisdom, infinite love, infinite virtue, infinite truth, infinite glory, etc.—and through these God appears to have several descriptions, we nevertheless set down only one here, which is this: **God is a being needing nothing outside Himself, but every other being needs Him.**

This is clear because He is superior to all **entity** <note>original: *entitate*; the very essence or state of being.</note>. By this description or definition, God is distinguished from every other being that exists; for indeed, all other beings need something outside themselves. Consequently, in Him there is no **contrariety** <note>original: *contrarietas*; the conflict between opposing forces or elements.</note>, nor **minority**, nor any defective or **privative** principles <note>original: *priuatiua*; a philosophical term for the absence or lack of a quality that should naturally be present.</note>. This "majority" is expressed in respect to His creatures, while "equality" is expressed in respect to Himself. The second part appears...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of God's nature, focusing on how His infinite attributes (Goodness, Greatness, etc.) function through internal "relatives" or active and passive principles. This ensures that God's internal activity is infinite and eternal rather than "idle" or static.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

...[is evident], since He possesses equal principles: namely goodness, greatness, duration, power, wisdom, and so on. He possesses principles and equal acts, and equal relations. Yet in God, there is a **differentiation of relatives** <note>original: *differentia relatiuorũ*; referring to the distinct roles within a single attribute, such as the power that acts and the power that is acted upon, which allows for dynamic internal life within the Godhead.</note> without which they cannot exist, nor could God have an **intrinsic action** <note>original: *actionem intrinsecam*; an internal activity within the Divine nature, such as the Trinity's internal relations, as opposed to external actions like creating the world.</note> that is permanent and infinite. Indeed, without these intrinsic relatives, all actions in God would be **idle** <note>original: *ociosę*; meaning inactive, useless, or without purpose—a state considered absurd and impossible for a perfect being.</note>, which is absurd.

This is also clear because, through **goodness**, He possesses the <term>bonifier</term>, the <term>bonifiable</term>, and the act of <term>bonifying</term> <note>original: *bonificatiuum, bonificabile, & bonificare*; these are Ramon Llull’s specialized Latin terms used to describe how an attribute functions: there is an active force, a receptive capacity, and the action itself, all existing as one within the Divine essence.</note>. These relatives are **co-essential** with God Himself; both **Deity** and **Goodness** are the same in Him. Similarly, there is the **understander**, the **understandable**, and the act of **understanding**. Therefore, etc.

Furthermore, in God there is **concordance**; this is clear, for through it He is infinitely distant from **contrariety** <note>original: *contrarietate*; conflict or opposition between different forces.</note>, and His relatives agree infinitely and eternally in one essence and a nature of identity. Because of this, it can also be said of His internal actions that in God there is no **quantity**, nor **quality**, nor **time**. This is clear because His essence would be infinitely **bare of every accident** <note>original: *nuda ab omni accidẽte*; in Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a property that can change without changing the nature of the thing (like hair color). Because God is perfect and unchanging, He has no accidents; His attributes are His essence.</note>. Therefore, etc.
