<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the human intellect understands the nature of beings. Having established that God possesses internal "relatives" (active and passive principles) to ensure His activity is eternal, the text now applies this logic to the human intellect's relationship with "separate substances" (angels) and how we can deduce God's superior nature by comparing Him to these high-ranking beings.</meta>

...to know, to understand, and to join or unite itself with the **separate substances** <note>original: *substantiis separatis*; in medieval philosophy, these are beings existing without physical bodies, specifically angels or pure intellects.</note> in which its **blessedness** <note>original: *beatitudo*; the state of supreme spiritual happiness or "heavenly bliss."</note> consists. And it furthermore knows those things which can be predicated of these separate substances through the principles and rules attributed to them.

Further, the human intellect knows that if an angel and other subjects have natural power in themselves, then much more—and by far—does God have it, since He is a subject not only higher and nobler than the others, but indeed the highest and noblest, as is evident through the **argument from the greater to the lesser** <note>original: *locum maioris ad minus*; a logical principle stating that if a quality exists in a lesser being, it must exist even more perfectly in a greater being.</note>.

And the angel is also deducible through all the principles and rules appropriated to it; for it has natural goodness, greatness, eternity, power, wisdom, and so on for the others. This angel can be defined in this way: namely, an **Angel** is an intellectual substance very much like God; for in it there is a nature of **bonifying**, **magnifying**, **eternizing**, and so forth. This is because it has its own **essential relatives**, namely the <term>bonifier</term>, the <term>bonifiable</term>, and the act of <term>bonifying</term> <note>original: *bonificatiuum, bonificabile, & bonificare*; Llull’s specialized terms for the active principle, the receptive principle, and the resulting action within a single quality.</note>. Likewise, the <term>magnifier</term>, the <term>magnifiable</term>, and the act of <term>magnifying</term>, which are signified through the **second species of Rule C** <note>In Ramon Llull's "Art," Rule C (the rule of "Why?") explains how a thing is composed of these three internal "relatives" to allow for internal activity.</note>; and in the angel there is also **majority** <note>original: *maioritas*; in this context, it refers to the principle of "Greatness" or "Preeminence" among the attributes.</note>...

<summary>This page explains how the human intellect uses the "separate substances" (angels) as a bridge to understand God. It defines an angel as a being that mirrors God's internal structure of active and passive principles (like "bonifying" and "magnifying") across its various attributes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical progression from the previous page, moving from the definition of an angel to the comparison between angelic and human capabilities. <page-num>35</page-num>
<sig>E iij</sig>

...it is evident, since the angel is greater than man, and therefore greater and higher principles apply to it, as well as higher rules, in comparison to the principles and rules that apply to man. In this step, the intellect recognizes that even if a human being cannot use the **powers of the soul** <note>original: *potentiis animæ*; the capacities for thought, will, and memory.</note> without a physical organ <note>The medieval view held that human thought requires the brain and senses, whereas angelic thought does not.</note>, it does not follow that an angel cannot. This is because the term **"power"** <note>original: *potentia*</note> is used **equivocally** <note>original: *æquiuocè*; a logical term meaning the same word is being used to describe two very different things.</note> when referring to the power of a human soul and the power of an angel.

Therefore, an angel can communicate its own concepts and act within us without possessing a physical organ of its own.

Furthermore, there is **difference** <term>Difference (differentia): The principle that allows for distinction; here, it refers to the distinct "compartments" of the angelic mind.</term> within the angel: this is clear because its intellect, will, and memory are distinct from one another. There is also within the angel an **equality** <term>Equality (æqualitas): A state of balance; in this context, it refers to the angel’s ability to direct its faculties toward God with equal intensity.</term> of understanding, loving, and remembering, by reason of the supreme subject <note>God</note> toward whom it is equally lovable, intelligible, and worthy of remembrance.

Moreover, there is **minority** <note>original: *minoritas*; the quality of being lesser or lower in rank.</note> in the angel; this is evident because it is lesser than God. For this reason, the first intelligence <note>The highest rank of celestial being.</note> is greater than the second, the second greater than the third, the third than the fourth, the fourth than the fifth, the fifth than the sixth, and the sixth than the seventh, and so on successively down to the **human intellect**, which is the last and lowest of the intelligences, and which is the extreme...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hierarchy of being, shifting from the nature of individual angels to their role in the cosmic order. It specifically addresses how "intelligences" (angelic beings) act as the moving forces behind the celestial spheres. Note: The page numbering in the source jumps from 35 to 28, suggesting a possible misbinding or separate section in the original manuscript.</meta>

...just as God is the other extreme of the **intelligences** <term>Intelligences (intelligentiæ): In medieval cosmology, these are purely spiritual beings—often identified as angels—that direct the movements of the planets and stars.</term> above, it is clearly evident that the intermediate intelligences are the movers of the celestial bodies. For this reason, it appears that if the order of nature allows for one intelligence that does not move a celestial body at all—neither by itself nor through another—it is necessary that there be another intelligence that moves the heavens through something else. This being does not move the heavens physically, but acts as a **non-moving mover** <note>original: *non mouens, nisi tanquam amatum, & desideratum*; a reference to Aristotle’s "Unmoved Mover," which inspires motion in others through their desire for its perfection.</note>, moved only as a beloved and desired object. Let these remarks suffice regarding the angel.

Indeed, **Heaven** <note>original: *Cœlum*; here referring to the physical universe and the celestial spheres.</note> possesses natural goodness, greatness, power, duration, and wisdom—or rather, the capacity to be known—as well as will. It also possesses virtue, truth, and glory in its own unique way, which is why it can be understood through all the established principles and rules of nature.

This "Heaven" is defined in the following way: Heaven is the **primary movable body** <note>In the Aristotelian view, the heavens are the first things set in motion by the divine.</note> in whose essence there is no **contrariety** <term>Contrariety (contrarietas): The state of being made of opposing elements (like hot and cold). Because the heavens were thought to be made of a "fifth element," they were believed to be free from the decay caused by such internal conflicts.</term>. Within heaven, there are natural appetites and instincts, and consequently, there is both the capacity for motion and the act of moving. Thus, it contains within itself a more perfect version of the **mover, the moved, and the motion** <note>original: *motiuum, mobile, & mouere*</note>. Without these three things, it could not possess a nature that is both infinite and perpetual. Because of this, heaven is the **efficient and productive cause** <term>Efficient Cause (causa efficiens): The primary source or agent that brings a change or an object into being.</term> of all things in the lower world. It is the active force within the **four elements** <note>Fire, Air, Water, and Earth.</note> and within all **material things** <note>original: *elementatis*; objects composed of the four elements.</note>...

Without these, the faculty cannot exist, and they are the components from which the **common sense** <note>original: *sensus communis*; the internal faculty that integrates information from the five external senses into a single experience</note> is constituted. 

The **sensitive power** <term>sensitive power: the faculty of physical sensation</term> is a potentiality in an animated body by which it sees colors, hears sounds, smells odors, tastes flavors, and perceives heat, cold, dryness, and moisture, as well as hardness, softness, roughness, and smoothness. This power finds its object in another—for instance, the color black in a raven, whiteness in snow, or flavor in a blended substance. 

The **sensitive power** also possesses general relatives through the **common sense**, and specific relatives through each particular sense. For example, through the sense of sight, there is the perceiver (*the visual*), the perceived (*the visible*), and the act of seeing; through hearing, there is the listener (*the auditory*), the heard (*the audible*), and the act of hearing. This logic applies to the other three senses in their own respective ways. 

Furthermore, the **sensitive faculty** is rooted and intertwined within the **vegetable** <note>The "vegetable" or vegetative level refers to the biological life of plants, which focuses on growth and nutrition.</note> just as the **vegetative faculty** is within elemental matter. The **sensitive faculty** is the fulfillment and the active expression <term>perfection and act: terms from Aristotelian philosophy meaning the realization or "functioning" of a potential ability</term> of a living animal body. By means of this, the animal perceives through touch and its other sensory objects.

<meta>This page continues the technical definition of the vegetative power, specifically its dependency on lower elemental structures and its role as a foundation for higher sensitive powers. It also introduces the definition of the "elemental" (elementatiuum) as the base of physical existence.</meta>

...[per]fection of a living body. By reason of this perfection, the living body acts only to produce what is similar to itself—whether in genus, in species, or in its very essence. It is the power of the soul by which a **physical body** <term>physical body: the material aspect of a living thing</term> is nourished and grows, and by which its species is preserved as a unified whole. 

This **vegetative power** <note>original: *vegetativa*</note> has its own essential "correlates" <note>In the philosophical system of Ramon Llull, every power has a triad: the active doer, the passive object, and the act itself. For example, the power to love involves a lover, a beloved, and the act of loving.</note> from which it derives its being and its ability to function. These are: the **vegetative** (the active force), the **vegetable** (the object being acted upon), and **vegetating** (the action itself). This power finds its foundation in the **elemental** <term>elemental: the level of inanimate matter composed of the four elements (earth, air, fire, water)</term>, and it serves as the foundation for the **sensitive power** <term>sensitive power: the faculty of sensation and movement found in animals</term>. 

For this reason, if the vegetative power is destroyed, the sensitive power is also destroyed; and if the elemental foundation is destroyed, the vegetative power is destroyed. Through the use of seed—or something else serving the purpose of a seed—the vegetative power procreates its likeness within its species. Just as we have given examples regarding the categories of **Rule C** <note>One of the logical rules in Llull's "Art," used to investigate the nature of subjects</note>, the same should be done for the other rules. 

Therefore, etc. In the **elemental**, there are likewise principles and rules by which it is guided. Under this category, many things are distinguished and organized by species, such as **gold**, **silver**, and other **metals**, as well as **stones** and similar things. <meta>This page continues the technical classification of the "powers" or principles in Ramon Llull's logical system. The essential is like the eye, the hand, and such; the accidental instrument is like heat, cold, and similar things. Other instruments or other species of instrumentality are found in the chapter for the letter K.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "instrumental power," specifically how it manifests in moral and natural "habits" or dispositions. It concludes the list of virtues and vices before transitioning to a new section on "application."</meta>

For the **instrumental power** is the perfection and the act of the instrument, by reason of which the instrument—or that which is "instrumented"—acts only either virtuously or viciously. I say that to act "morally" happens in two ways: through nature, as in the **prudence** of a snake or the **magnanimity** <note>original: *magnanimitas*; the virtue of being great-souled or having high-minded courage</note> of a lion; or through acquisition, such as **justice**, **prudence**, **fortitude**, **temperance**, **faith**, **hope**, **charity**, **patience**, **piety**, **magnanimity**, and **liberality**.

All of these are habits acquired in humans through the **law of nature**, and for this reason, a human being separated from the law of nature is the worst of all animals <note>This echoes Aristotle’s *Politics*, which argues that humans, when perfected, are the best of animals, but when separated from law and justice, are the worst.</note>. Furthermore, the **privations** <term>privation: the absence or lack of a quality that should naturally be present (Latin: *priuatio*)</term> of these virtues are the vices: such as **injustice**; **avarice**, which is the privation of liberality; **gluttony**, which is the privation of sobriety; **lust**, which is the privation of chastity; and **pride**, which is the privation of humility; and so on for other species of vices. The definitions of these are left out for the sake of the brevity of this work. Wherefore, etc.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the "Art" or method of reasoning, moving from the definitions of habits and virtues into the practical "application" of its logical principles. It explains how to move between abstract concepts and concrete reality.</meta>

...while moving from the **implicit** to the **explicit**, or from the **abstract** to the **concrete**, or from a **question** to its proper place.

An example of the first type of application occurs when the terms of a question are **implicit** <term>implicit: implied or hidden within a concept without being stated (Latin: *implicitus*)</term> and are applied to the **explicit** <term>explicit: clearly and distinctly stated (Latin: *explicitus*)</term> terms of this teaching. For instance, [the question] of whether **God** exists, or an **angel**, or a **human**; and so it is with other subjects which are applied to **goodness**, **greatness**, **permanence**, **power**, and the other primary principles of this **wisdom** <note>This refers to the "General Art" of Ramon Llull, which uses a set of fundamental divine attributes (principles) to investigate all reality.</note>. These include the being of God, the being of an angel, the being of the heavens, the being of a human, the being of the imaginative power, the being of the sensory power, and so forth.

An example of the second application is when the **abstract** terms of a question are applied to their **concrete** counterparts. For example:
- **Goodness** is applied to **that which is good**, 
- **Greatness** to **that which is great**, 
- **Duration** or **permanence** to **that which is permanent**, 
- **Eternity** to **that which is eternal**, 
- **Godhood** <note>original: *deitas*</note> to **God**, 
- **Angelicity** <note>original: *angeleitas*; the essential quality or "angel-ness" of an angel</note> to an **angel**, 
- **Heavenliness** <note>original: *cœlicitas*</note> to **heaven**, 
- **Humanity** <note>original: *homeitas*; the essence of being human</note> to a **human**, 
- and **heat** is applied to **that which is colored** <note>original: *coloratum*; this likely should be *calidum* (that which is hot), as heat usually relates to the hot in these logical pairings, though it may refer to the "coloring" or sensible quality of a substance</note>.

And so it goes for other things in their own way. One must always examine the manner in which **abstract terms** relate to **concrete ones**, as they are led through the **principles** toward the **rules**. Furthermore, it should be noted...

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the "modes of application" within the Lullian system of logic. It lists the nine specific ways a researcher can apply a question to the various components of the "Art"—such as its figures, principles, and rules—ensuring the logical framework remains intact before transitioning to the final section of the work regarding the "quiddities" or essential natures of things.</meta>

Moreover, the third mode of application is divided into nine species, of which the first concerns the **first figure** <note>In the Art of Ramon Llull, the "First Figure" (Figure A) typically represents the divine attributes or principles like Goodness, Greatness, and Power arranged in a circle.</note>. The second concerns the **second figure**. The third concerns the **third**, and the fourth concerns the **fourth figure**. 

The fifth species of applying a question to its proper place concerns the **combination of principles and rules**. The sixth concerns the **rules**. The seventh concerns the **nine subjects** <note>The nine subjects in this system usually include God, Angel, Heaven, Human, Imaginative, Sensitive, Vegetative, Elementative, and Instrumentative.</note>. The eighth species of applying questions to their proper place concerns the **quiddities** <term>quiddity: the "whatness" or the essential nature that makes a thing what it is (Latin: *quiditas*)</term>, or the **haecceity** <term>haecceity: the "thisness" or the specific quality that makes an individual thing unique from all others (Latin: *haecheitas*)</term> of the **hundred forms** <note>This refers to the "One Hundred Forms," a specific list of concepts (like "Justice," "Quantity," or "Time") used in Lullian philosophy to categorize all reality.</note>. And the ninth species concerns the **questions** themselves. 

Regarding these, however, it must be noted that the terms are applied according to the mode of the question (or questions) in a way that is appropriate to them. For example, if the subject matter of a question is appropriate to the **first figure**, let it be applied to that figure. This should be done so that the solution to the question—whether by affirming or denying—leaves the "text" of that figure unharmed <note>original: *remaneat illasus*; meaning the logical application must not contradict the fundamental definitions or internal consistency of the system's diagrams.</note>. And just as we have provided an example of this using the first figure, you should consider us to have given examples for the others in their own respective ways. Let these remarks suffice concerning application for the sake of brevity. Therefore, etc.

->*The third and final treatise is concerning the means to the sought-after end in this wisdom, and first concerning the quiddities of forms.*<-

<meta>This page continues the transition from the "modes of application" to the discussion of essential natures (quiddities). It uses the analogy of physical change (generation and destruction) to explain how human knowledge is formed in the mind through logical demonstration.</meta>

Since it is indeed inherent in the nature of things that can be **generated** <term>generation: the process of coming into being or being created</term> and **corrupted** <term>corruption: the process of decaying, breaking down, or ceasing to exist</term> first to separate the parts of anything corruptible. Second, to bring together the parts of anything that can be generated, as is evident in every instance of generation and corruption. 

The causes of these processes are **strife** and **friendship** <note>original: "lis, & amicitia." These terms refer to the ancient philosophical theory of Empedocles, who argued that Love (attraction) and Strife (repulsion) are the two forces that mix and separate the elements to create the world.</note>: for strife in things is the cause of stripping forms away from the subject, and love is the cause of their union with it. For that which love generates or unites is a **composite of matter and form** <note>A core idea in Aristotelian philosophy (hylomorphism) where everything is made of "stuff" (matter) and "structure" (form).</note>, and that which strife destroys is likewise a composite of matter and form. 

And this occurs in sensible things <note>things that can be perceived by the five senses</note> for the most part; because sensible things behave this way in generation and corruption, so too do **intelligible things** <note>concepts or ideas that are grasped by the mind rather than the senses</note> behave in the process of cognition or understanding. For this reason, knowledge is not generated within us except through the **destruction of ignorance**. For everything is destroyed through separation and is generated through aggregation [gathering together]. From this, it appears that knowledge is not generated in us except through the gathering together of the **major extremity** with the **minor** <term>major and minor extremes: the two main terms in a logical syllogism (like "Socrates" and "mortal") that are joined by a "middle term" to reach a conclusion</term> through the "middle" of a **demonstration** <term>demonstration: a logical proof that leads to certain knowledge</term>, which is the very **essence** <note>original: "quod quid est," literally "the what it is," referring to the quiddity or definition of a thing</note> of the thing itself. It is necessary, there—

<summary>This page explains that human learning mirrors the physical world: just as nature creates and destroys through "love" and "strife," the mind creates knowledge by destroying ignorance and joining logical terms together through demonstration.</summary>
<keywords>generation and corruption, strife and friendship, matter and form, cognition, logic, syllogism, quiddity, Empedocles</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical application of the concepts of "separation" and "gathering" introduced on the previous page. Having established that physical things are created or destroyed through these forces, the author now applies these mechanics to the human mind's ability to define and understand the essence of things.</meta>

...ring. We must first investigate it according to the **divisive method** <term>divisive method: a logical approach that breaks down a broad category into its specific parts to find a specific definition</term>, which is very similar to separation. Afterward, however, we must investigate it according to the **compositive method** <term>compositive method: a logical approach that builds up a complex concept from simpler, constituent parts</term>, which is very similar to gathering. 

For indeed, our knowledge of the thing will follow from this: the separation of one thing is the gathering of another. For example, if a human being is not irrational, it follows that they are rational, since it is manifest that they are an **animal** <note>In medieval philosophy, 'animal' simply refers to any living being with the power of sensation, not necessarily a "beast."</note>, because they possess sensation. But every sensible thing is an animal; therefore it is plain that a human being is a **rational animal**, or a "discursive" rational being <note>original: "rationale discursiuile." This refers to the human ability to move the mind from one premise to a conclusion, "running" through an argument.</note>.

Thus, it is manifest that to investigate the **essence** <note>original: "quod quid est," literally translated as "the what-it-is," referring to the essential nature of a thing.</note>, one must first use the divisive science. And since this essence is nothing other than the **definition**, and every definition expresses only the "whatness" of a thing's being—and the whole being of a thing comes from its **form** <term>form: the internal structural principle or essence that makes a thing what it is, as opposed to the physical matter it is made of</term>—it follows that a definition is nothing other than the form. 

But every form of a thing is its **quiddity** <term>quiddity: from the Latin "quidditas," meaning the "what-ness" or the specific essence that makes a thing what it is</term>; it follows that the "what-it-is" is the quiddity of the thing. Therefore, it is clear that for a true knowledge of the essence, it is helpful to abstract the form of the thing from the thing itself. One must begin from what is most known to the intellect, and this is nothing other than the...

<summary>This page explains the logical methods of division and composition used to arrive at a definition. It argues that a thing's true essence or "quiddity" is synonymous with its "form," which the mind must abstract to achieve true understanding.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the definition of things, moving from the "compositive method" to the metaphysical relationship between existence, essence, and unity. It explains how these abstract concepts relate to simple and complex beings.</meta>

...existence <note>original: "ipsum esse"</note> whose act and whose form is the **essence** itself. Therefore, essence is the act and the perfection of existence itself, by reason of which existence does nothing but exist, or rather, produces existence; and for this reason existence is inseparable from things.

**Unity** is the act and the perfection of the "one" <note>original: "unius"</note>, by reason of which the one does nothing but act as one. From this it follows that from the one, insofar as it is one, nothing proceeds but the one. Furthermore, it is clear from this that unity is the cause of **composition** <term>composition: the logical or physical putting together of different parts into a whole</term>; while its opposite, multiplicity, is the cause [of division]. 

For this reason, multiplicity is always divisible, and unity is always indivisible. Hence, it is repugnant to the divine nature to say that unity is divisible, insofar as it is a **simple unity** <note>original: "unitas simplex," referring to a unity that is not made of parts and cannot be broken down</note>. Therefore, etc. 

From these things it also appears that **being** <term>being: in Latin, *ens*, referring to an existing entity or thing that "is"</term> is predicated <note>predicated: a logical term meaning to state or assert a quality about a subject</note> of something simple, though not of everything; and it is also predicated of the "one," though not of every one, but of the **mixed**. Indeed, it is always predicated of every mixed thing and every **composite** thing according to the "before and after" <note>original: "secundum prius & posterius," a logical concept where one thing is considered more fundamental or prior to another</note>. This is because it is predicated first of the simple and later of the mixed, and it is predicated first of the one and later of the many or the manifold.

And for this reason, within these terms, the human **intellect** completes the attainment of its conce—

<meta>This page continues the logical definitions of metaphysical concepts, moving from "nature" on the previous page to "substance," "beginning-ness," "causality," and "necessity." This is evident because if substance were set apart and stripped <note>original: "expoliata"</note> of all its **accidents** <term>accidents: non-essential properties, such as color, shape, or weight, which can change without the underlying thing losing its identity</term>, it would still be good; this would not be the case if substance were not the total goodness of the thing and the total good being of the thing. Wherefore, etc.

These are also deducible through all the principles and rules, just as goodness, greatness, and other such things are deducible. 

**Beginning-ness** <note>original: "initieitas"</note> is the act of that which is begun; by reason of this, a begun thing acts only as a begun thing. Therefore, it is the beginning from whose being everything proceeds, and before it, there is nothing. Hence, the **initiating**, the **initiable**, and **to initiate** are its correlatives, without which it cannot exist. 

**Causality** <note>original: "causeitas, sive causalitas"</note> is the act and perfection of a cause; by reason of this, a cause acts only as a cause. Or rather, it does not act as the **effect** <note>original: "causatum"</note>, and its whole being is found within its correlatives, encompassing the **four types of causes** <note>The Aristotelian causes: material, formal, efficient, and final</note>.

<meta>This page continues the series of metaphysical definitions following the Lullian system. It moves from the definitions of "substance" and "causality" on the previous page to "indivisibility," "element-ness," "identity," and "likeness."</meta>

That whose being is immutable also has its own **correlatives** <term>correlatives: in this philosophical system, every essence is expressed through a triad: the active doer (-ive), the passive recipient (-able), and the action itself (the verb)</term> in which its being is comprised.

**Indivisibility** <note>original: "indivisibilitas"</note> is the act and perfection of the undivided; by reason of which the undivided acts only as the undivided. Moreover, the undivided is a being from whose being nothing is cut off, nor can be cut off. It has its essential correlatives, without which it cannot exist: namely, the **undividing**, the **undividable**, and **to undivide**.

**Element-ness** <note>original: "elementeitas"</note> is the act and perfection of an element; by reason of which an element acts only as an element. It has its essential correlatives, namely: the **elementing**, the **elementable**, and **to element**. An **element** is a being into whose essence all things are resolved <note>original: "resolvuntur"; in medieval science, this refers to breaking a compound down into its simplest parts</note>, while it itself is resolved into nothing further.

**Identity** <note>original: "identitas"</note> is the act and perfection of the "same"; by reason of which the same acts only as the same. Therefore, the "same" is a being whose existence performs nothing upon the One except a relation. It has its correlatives: the **identifying**, the **identifiable**, and **to identify**.

**Likeness-ness** <note>original: "similitudineitas"</note> is the act and perfection of the "like"; by reason of which the like acts only as the like. Moreover, the "like" is a being which expresses nothing but a relation of parity <note>original: "aequiparantiae"; a logical term for a relationship where both sides are equal or similar in kind</note>. It has its essential correlatives—

**First-ness** <note>original: "primieitas"; a Lullian neologism referring to the essence of being "first"</note> is the act and perfection of the first; by reason of which the first acts only as the first. Moreover, the first is a being whose existence lacks any superior <note>original: "superiori"; meaning nothing precedes it in hierarchy or cause</note>; and it has its **first-ing**, **first-able**, and **to first**.

**Potency-ness** <note>original: "potentieitas"</note> is the act of potency <note>or "power"</note>; by reason of which potency acts only as potency. Therefore, potency is a being whose existence is indifferent <note>meaning it has the capacity for both</note> toward acting or being acted upon, and it has its correlatives: namely, the **potency-ing**, the **potency-able**, and **to potency**. 

**Actuality-ness** <note>original: "actueitas"</note> is the act and perfection of the actualized; by reason of which the actualized acts only as the actualized. Therefore, the actualized is a being whose existence is complete in itself, and its correlatives are well-known <note>the author implies the reader can now predict the pattern: the actuating, the actuatable, and to actuate</note>. 

**Quantity**, or **Quantity-ness** <note>original: "quantieitas"</note> is the act of the quantitative; by reason of which the quantitative acts only as the quantitative. And it has its correlatives: namely, the **quantity-ing**, the **quantity-able**, and **to quantity**. Moreover, the quantitative is a being divisible into infinity. 

**Quality**, or **Quality-ness** <note>original: "qualieitas"</note> is the act of the qualified; by reason of which the qualified acts only as the qualified. Its correlatives are the **qualifying**, the **qualifiable**, and **to qualify**.

Its correlatives are the **perfecting**, namely the **perfectible**, and **to perfect**. Moreover, the **perfect** is a being in whose existence all perfections are found.

**Finitude-ness** <note>original: "finieitas"</note> is the act of the finite, by reason of which the finite acts only as the finite. Its correlatives are the **finiting**, namely the **finitable**, and **to finite**. Furthermore, the **finite** is a being whose existence is contained within certain bounds and limits.

**Wholeness** or **Totality** <note>original: "toteitas" or "totalitas"</note> is the act of the whole, by reason of which the whole acts only as the whole. Now, the **whole** is a being from whose existence nothing is missing; and it has its own correlatives: the **wholing**, namely the **wholable**, and **to whole**.

**Diminished-ness** <note>original: "diminueitas"</note> is the act of the diminished, by reason of which the diminished acts only as the diminished. Moreover, the **diminished** is a being whose existence is imperfect; and it has its correlatives: the **diminishing**, namely the **diminishable**, and **to diminish**.

**Genus-ness** or **Generality** <note>original: "genereitas" or "generalitas"</note> is the act of the genus, by reason of which the genus acts only as the genus. <meta>This page continues the definitions of metaphysical concepts in Ramon Llull's "Art," picking up from the end of the definition of Generality on the previous page. It defines Species, Individuality, Personality, Haecceity (this-ness), and begins the definition of Other-ness.</meta>

Moreover, the **species** is a being whose existence encompasses individuals of one nature, although there may be some species whose existence encompasses only one individual and is equal to it—just as a container is equal to its content, or as with the sun and an angel. <note>In medieval thought, unique beings like the sun or specific angels were often considered to be the sole members of their own species.</note>

**Individual-ness** or **Individuality** <note>original: "indiuidueitas" or "indiuidualitas"</note> is the act and perfection of the individual, by reason of which the individual acts only as an individual. **Person-ness** or **Personality** <note>original: "personeitas" or "personalitas"</note> is the act and perfection of the person, by reason of which the person acts only as a person. <note>The source text repeats "acts" ("non agit agit"), likely a scribal error for the standard formula "acts only as."</note> Moreover, the **person** is a being whose existence is an **individual substance of a rational nature** <note>original: "substantia rationalis naturae"; the classic philosophical definition of a person</note>, and it has its own known correlatives <note>referring to the personifying power, the personifiable, and the act of personifying</note>.

**Haecceity** or **"This-ness"** <note>original: "hecheitas" or "haecceitas"; a term for the discrete qualities that make a thing uniquely "this" thing and not "that" thing</note> is the act of "this," by reason of which "this" acts only as "this." Now, **this** is a being whose existence demonstrates <note>or "points out"</note> something, and it has its **haecceity-ing**, **haecceity-able**, and **to haecceity** <note>technical terms for the active, passive, and verbal expressions of this-ness</note>, which are its correlatives.

<meta>This page continues the technical definitions of metaphysical categories within Ramon Llull’s logical system. It completes the definition of "Otherness" from the previous page and proceeds to define Subsistence, Accidentality, Agency, Actuality, and begins the definition of Passivity.</meta>

...[another] through which **another** does not act or produce except [as] another. Moreover, **another** <note>original: "aliud"</note> is a being whose existence is singular, and it has its correlatives: the **othering**, namely the **otherable**, and **to other**. <note>original: "alieitiuum, alieitabile, & aliei-tare." Llull frequently coins these terms to describe the three-part internal structure of any concept: the doer, the receiver, and the act itself.</note>

**Subsistence-ness** <note>original: "subsistentieitas"</note> is the formal act of the **subsisting [thing]**, by reason of which the subsisting thing acts only as a subsisting thing. Moreover, a **subsisting thing** is a being whose existence is said to be neither in a subject nor of a subject; <note>This uses the classic Aristotelian definition of "Substance": something that exists in its own right (like a tree) rather than as a property of something else (like the greenness of the tree).</note> and it has its correlatives: the **subsisting**, the **subsistable**, and **to subsist**.

**Accident-ness**, or **Accidality** <note>original: "accidentieitas, siue accidentalitas"</note>, is the act of the **accident**, by reason of which the accident acts only as an accident. An **accident** is therefore a being whose existence inheres in **primary substance**, or is a power originating from substance; <note>An "accident" in medieval logic is a quality (like color, weight, or shape) that cannot exist without a substance to "hold" it.</note> and it has its correlatives: the **accident-making**, the **accidentable**, and **to make an accident**.

**Agency-ness** <note>original: "agieitas"</note> is the act of the **agent**, by reason of which the agent acts only as an agent. Moreover, an **agent** is a being whose existence is moved by an **end**, <note>original: "finis"; referring to a goal, purpose, or final cause.</note> and it has its correlatives: the **acting-power**, namely the **actable**, and the **agent**.

**Actuality-ness** <note>original: "actueitas"</note> is the act of the **actuated [thing]**, by reason of which the actuated thing acts only as an actuated thing. Moreover, the **actuated thing** is a being whose existence is in its perfected state of being; and it has its correlatives: the **actuating**, the **actuable**, and **to actuate**.

**Passivity-ness** or **Passibility** <note>original: "passiueitas siue passibilitas"</note> is the act of the **patient** <note>original: "passi"; in this context, the "patient" is the thing that is acted upon or receives an action from an agent.</note>, by reason of which the patient acts only as...

<summary>This page defines a series of metaphysical "essences" (Otherness, Subsistence, Accidentality, Agency, and Actuality), explaining how each category functions through its own internal "acts" and Lullian correlatives.</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, Ramon Llull, substance, accident, agency, actuality, teleology, correlatives, scholasticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the definitions of metaphysical categories within Ramon Llull’s "Art." It completes the definition of the Passive/Patient category and provides definitions for Habit, Position, Time, and Motion. The text follows Llull’s characteristic structure of defining a category's "act" and its three "correlatives" (the active agent, the passive recipient, and the action itself).</meta>

**...passivity**. A **passive thing** <note>original: "passum." This refers to the object that receives an action, completing the definition from the previous page.</note> is therefore a being whose existence is always receptive, and it has its correlatives: the **passive**, namely the **passible**, and **to undergo**. <note>original: "passiuum, passibile, & pati." In Llull’s system, every reality is composed of these three functional parts: the power to do, the power to receive, and the act of doing.</note>

**Habit-ness** <note>original: "habitueitas siue habitualitas"</note> or **habituality** is the act of the **habituated**, by reason of which the habituated [thing] acts only as habituated. A **habituated thing** is therefore a being whose existence is acquired, and it has the **habituating**, the **habituable**, and **to habituate**. <note>In medieval philosophy, a "habit" (habitus) is not just a routine, but a stable quality or "garment" of the soul or body, such as knowledge or a physical disposition.</note>

**Position-ness** <note>original: "situeitas"</note> is the act of the **positioned**, by reason of which the positioned [thing] acts only as positioned. Moreover, a **positioned thing** is a being in whose existence there is a proper arrangement of all its own parts, and it has the **positioning**, the **positionable**, and **to position**, which are its intrinsic correlatives.

**Time-ness** <note>original: "temporeitas siue temporalitas"</note> or **temporality** is the act of time, by reason of which time acts only as time. Moreover, **time** is a being whose existence is the measure of motion, or the number of motion, <note>This follows the classic Aristotelian definition: time is how we count or measure the "before" and "after" of movement.</note> and it has its correlatives: the **temporizing**, namely the **temporizable**, and **to temporize**. <note>original: "temporatiuum, temporabile & temporare."</note>

**Motion-ness** <note>original: "motiuitas siue motus"</note> or **motion** is the act of the **moved**, by reason of which the moved [thing] acts only as moved. A **moved thing** is therefore a being whose existence is partly in the **point of origin** <note>original: "termino à quo"</note> and partly in the **point of destination** <note>original: "termino ad quem"</note>, and it is divisible into a part that moves by itself and a part that is moved by itself. And it has its correlatives, namely the **motive**, the **movable**, and **to move**.

**Place-ness...** <note>original catchword: "Locci-", which will likely be "Loccieitas" or "Loccitas" on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page defines the metaphysical categories of Passivity, Habit, Position, Time, and Motion using Ramon Llull's system of functional correlatives. Each category is defined by how it "acts" and the relationship between its active and passive internal components.</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, Ramon Llull, habit, position, time, motion, Aristotelian definitions, correlatives, point of origin, point of destination</keywords>

**Void-ness** <note>original: "vacuieitas"</note> is the act of the void, by reason of which the void acts only as the void. Moreover, a **void** is a being whose existence is space deprived of a body, and it has its correlatives: the **voiding**, namely the **voidable**, and **to void**. <note>Medieval philosophers debated whether a "vacuum" (void) could truly exist in nature, often defining it theoretically as space without physical matter.</note>

**Instinct-ness** <note>original: "instinctueitas"</note> is the act of instinct: by reason of which instinct acts only as instinct, and it has its known correlatives: the **instinct-making**, namely the **instinct-makable**, and **to make-instinct**. <note>original: "instinctuificativum, instinctuificabile, & instinctuificare." Llull frequently coined new Latin terms to ensure every concept fit his triadic model of "doer," "receiver," and "the act itself."</note>

**Appetite-ness** <note>original: "appetitivitas"</note> is the act of appetite, by reason of which appetite acts only as appetite: moreover, **appetite** is a being whose existence moves a power toward an object in which it naturally rests, and it has its essential correlatives: the **appetitive**, namely the **appetible**, and **to desire**. <note>In this context, "appetite" refers to any natural drive or inclination—such as a stone "desiring" to fall toward the earth—rather than just hunger.</note>

**Alteration** is the act of the **altered**: by reason of which the altered [thing] acts only as altered: Moreover, an **altered thing** is a being whose existence is tangible, and it has its correlatives: the **alterative**, namely the **alterable**, and **to alter**.

<meta>This page continues the systematic definitions of "acts" and "correlatives" following the Lullian method. **new-makable** <note>original: "nouificabile"</note>, and **to make new** <note>original: "nouificare"</note>.

**Idea-ness** <note>original: "ideietas"; a Lullian term for the essence of being an idea</note> is the act of the idea, by reason of which the **idea** acts only as an idea. Therefore, an **idea** is a being whose existence is imprinted in matter, and it has its intrinsic correlatives: the **idea-making** <note>original: "Ideitificatiuum"</note>, the **idea-makable** <note>original: "ideitificabile"</note>, and **to make an idea** <note>original: "ideitificare"</note>.

**Mathematics-ness** <note>original: "Mathematicheitas"</note> or **mathematics** is the act of the mathematical <note>original: "mathematici"; referring here to the mathematical principle or object</note>: by reason of which the **mathematical** acts only as something mathematical. Moreover, **mathematics** is a being whose existence is nothing other than form abstracted from motion; and it has its known correlatives: the **math-making**, the **math-makable**, and **to make mathematical**.

**Point-ness** <note>original: "Punctieitas"</note> or **punctuality** is the act of the point, by reason of which the **point** acts only as a point. Moreover, the **point** is a being whose existence is indivisible; and it is the beginning of a line, and it has its correlatives, namely: the **point-making**, the **point-makable**, and **to make points**.

**Line-ness** <note>original: "Linieitas"</note> is the act of the line, by reason of which the **line** acts only as a line. Moreover, the **line** is a magnitude that is only long, contained between two points.

**Triangle-ness** <note>original: "Triangulieitas"</note> is the act of the triangle, by reason of which the **triangle** acts only as a triangle. Moreover, the **triangle** is a being whose existence is contained by three lines, bounded by three points; its correlatives are the **triangle-making**...

It also has its correlatives <note>In Lullian philosophy, correlatives are the three components of any action: the doer, the receiver, and the act itself.</note>: the **quadrangle-making**, the **quadrangle-makable**, and **to make quadrangles**.

**Circle-ness** <note>original: "circuleitas"</note> or **circularity** is the act of the circle, by reason of which the **circle** acts only as a circle. Moreover, a **circle** is a being whose existence is enclosed by a single line drawn around a center, from which equal lines are drawn to the circumference of that line; it has its correlatives, namely: the **circle-making**, the **circle-makable**, and **to make circles**.

**Body-ness** <note>original: "corporeitas"</note> is the act of the body, by reason of which the **body** acts only as a body. Moreover, a **body** is a being whose existence is contained within three dimensions <note>length, width, and depth</note>; it has its correlatives, namely: the **body-making**, the **body-makable**, and **to make a body**.

**Figure-ness** <note>original: "figureitas"</note> or **figurality** is the act of the shaped thing, by reason of which the **shaped thing** <note>original: "figuratum"</note> acts only as a shaped thing. Moreover, a **monster** is an animated body whose existence is deficient in some natural boundary according to the nature of its species, occurring either always or frequently; it has its correlatives: the **monster-making**, the **monster-makable**, and **to make a monster** <note>original: "monstruofificatiuum, monstruofificabile, & monstruofificare"</note>.

And it should be noted briefly that all abstract forms of any concrete thing—that is, its **this-ness** <note>original: "hecheitas"; a philosophical term referring to the specific qualities that make an individual thing what it is</note>—are formed from its possessive case. For example, from the word for **shady** <note>original: "vmbrosum"</note>, the possessive form is "of the shady" <note>original: "vmbrosi"</note>; by adding the suffix "-ness," **shadiness** <note>original: "vmbrosieitas"</note> is formed. This is the act of the shady thing, by reason of which the **shady thing** acts only as a shady thing.

Moreover, a **shady thing** is a body whose existence is opaque, and it has its internal correlatives, namely: the **shade-making**, the **shade-makable**, and **to make shade** <note>original: "vmbrofificatiuum, vmbrofificabile, & vmbrofificare"</note>. Just as this example was given for the formation of the abstract form of "shady" itself, you should consider that I have given an example for all other concrete things. <meta>This page continues the catalog of definitions within the Lullian system. Having previously established the "correlatives" (the active, passive, and verbal principles of existence) for physical and abstract concepts, the author now applies this linguistic and metaphysical framework to a wide array of theological, scientific, and logical terms, framing them within a "Kabbalistic" or universal combinatorial method.</meta>

## Page 22
### C 3

**Kabbalah** <note>original: "Kabbala"; here referring to the Lullian method of combining divine names or attributes to understand the structure of reality</note> consists of these things, namely: **study**, **colored**, **proportioned**, **disposed**, **created**, **predestined**, **foreknown**, **merciful**, **necessitated**, **formed**, **fortunate**, **ordered**, **deliberated**, **generous**, **participated**, **perfect**, **declared**, **transubstantiated**, **altered**, **infinite**, **deceived**, **venerated**, **capable**, **existing**, **comprehended**, **found**, **souls**, **fitting**, **object**, **generated**, **conceived**, the **Theologian** or **theologizing**, the **Philosopher** or **philosophizing**, the **Mathematician** or **mathematizing**, the **Geometer**, the **Musician**, the **Arithmetician**, the **Astronomer**, the **Rhetorician**, the **Logician**, the **Grammarian**, the **Politician**, the **Jurist**, the **Physician**, **Governance**, **Judgment**, and so forth.

There are derivative names of every kind, and furthermore, whatever exists is categorized in the following ways:
It is either **in a subject** and **predicated of a subject** <note>original: "dicitur de subiecto"; a term from Aristotelian logic referring to how qualities or definitions relate to an individual thing</note>, such as **the capacity for laughter** or **laughter-ability** <note>original: "risibile siue risibilitas"; in medieval logic, this is the classic example of a property that belongs essentially to humans</note>;
or it is **in a subject** but **not predicated of a subject**, such as **color** <note>color exists within a body, but we do not say the body is "a color" itself</note>;
or it is **not in a subject** but **is predicated of a subject**, such as **animal** and **man** <note>these are universal categories of being (substances) rather than accidental qualities</note>;
or it is **neither in a subject** nor **predicated of a subject**, such as **Socrates** and **Plato** <note>individual persons/substances who exist independently and are not descriptors of anything else</note>.

<summary>This page lists various attributes, sciences, and professions that can be analyzed through the Lullian-Kabbalistic method and concludes with a summary of the four Aristotelian categories of being regarding subjects and predicates.</summary>

<keywords>Kabbalah, Lullism, classification of sciences, Aristotelian logic, subject and predicate, ontology, medieval professions</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general definitions of the Lullian system to "Part Two," which focuses on the "Questions" (Quaestiones) arising from the specific combinatory figures. # Part Two: On Questions, and First Regarding Figure A
->G iiij<- <note>Printer's signature marking the sequence of the book's quires</note>

In the first spherical figure, which is titled **A** <note>original: "figura A"; in Lullian logic, Figure A is a circular diagram representing the attributes of God—such as Goodness, Greatness, and Eternity—demonstrating how they are all interconnected and identical in the divine essence</note>, it is first asked whether, in the order of **nature** <note>original: "natura"; here referring to the fundamental structure of reality</note>, there exists some one thing in whose being the **subject** and **predicate** <term>subject and predicate: the two parts of a logical statement; for example, in "God is Good," God is the subject and Good is the predicate</term> are converted essentially and identically. 

To this, one must respond affirmatively. Otherwise, simple conversions <note>original: "conuersiones simplices"; a logical rule where the subject and predicate can be swapped (e.g., "No circle is a square" becomes "No square is a circle") without losing truth</note> and the equalities of things would be destroyed. Consequently, **eternity** would be superior to, and more common than, **goodness**, **greatness**, and **power** themselves through an infinite **duration**. 

If this were the case, some good thing might be eternal, and yet not every eternal thing would be a good thing, which is unfitting and contradictory. Similarly, every great thing would be eternal—or greatness would be eternal—and yet not every eternal thing would be great. Furthermore, every powerful thing would be eternal, yet not every eternal thing would be powerful; in the same way, every **wise** person would be eternal, yet not every eternal thing would be wise. Many other inconveniences and impossibilities would follow if, in the order of nature, there were not some one thing in whose being these attributes are identical.

Secondly, it is asked: what is that one thing?

<summary>This page introduces the logical application of Figure A (Divine Attributes), arguing that in the ultimate reality (God), attributes like Goodness, Greatness, and Eternity must be identical and interchangeable. The author uses logical "conversion" to prove that if these attributes were not equal, it would lead to the impossible conclusion that something could be eternal without being good or wise.</summary>

<keywords>Figure A, Divine Dignities, Logic, Conversion, Eternity, Goodness, Essential Identity, Predicate, Subject</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical inquiry into "Figure A" from the previous page, specifically addressing the identity and equality of God’s attributes—such as Goodness, Action, and Will—to ensure that no hierarchy or lack exists within the divine nature.</meta>

...in which, or in whose being, the **subject** and **predicate** <term>subject and predicate: the two parts of a logical statement; in the phrase "God is Great," God is the subject and Great is the predicate</term> are converted <note>original: "conuertuntur"; in logic, this means the terms can be swapped (e.g., "God is Good" and "Goodness is God") because they refer to the same infinite essence</note>, as stated above. To which it must be responded that this being is **God**. It is evident, since such conversion and equality cannot exist except in the one infinite being, who is superior even to eternity itself.

It is asked **thirdly** whether the **divine goodness** has a great **bonification** <term>bonification: a specific term in Lullian philosophy (original: "bonificatio") referring to the internal "doing" or active functioning of goodness within God</term>, just as His **intellect** has a great intellection. To this, it must be answered affirmatively. Otherwise, it would follow that in God there would be something inferior and something superior, which is absurd <note>Scholastic philosophy held that God is "simple," meaning He has no parts and no internal hierarchy; all His attributes are equally supreme</note>.

**Fourthly**, it is asked whether God has within Himself as much **action** as He has **essence**. To this, it must be answered affirmatively. Otherwise, He would act less than He is able, which is impossible.

And it is asked **fifthly** whether God knows all things just as His **essence** encompasses all things. To this, it must be answered affirmatively; otherwise, His **goodness** would not be communicated to all beings, and thus there would be some being in nature that is not good, which is patently false. 

It is asked **sixthly** whether God is a **voluntary agent** <term>voluntary agent: a being that acts through free will rather than by being forced or moving by natural necessity</term>. To this, it must be answered affirmatively. Otherwise, no agent would be free, but every being would be bound <note>original: "alligatum"; meaning constrained or restricted by necessity</note>, and thus no good would be given...

<summary>This page uses Lullian logic to argue that God's attributes—Goodness, Essence, Action, and Will—are all perfectly equal and infinite. It concludes that God must be a voluntary agent, as a lack of free will would result in a universe governed by necessity rather than goodness.</summary>

...by essence, neither great, nor powerful, nor eternal, nor wise, which is inconsistent <note>original: "inconueniens"; in medieval logic, this refers to a logical impossibility or a contradiction that breaks the rules of the system</note>. 

It is asked **seventhly** whether God is **virtuous**, to which it must be responded affirmatively; otherwise, infinite **goodness** in all beings would not be virtuous but vicious, and thus good would be evil and contradictory to itself, which is inconsistent and absurd. Therefore, etc. 

And it is asked **eighthly** whether God is **true**, to which it must be responded affirmatively. Otherwise, there would be no truth, which is because truth is the very act of being true <note>The author argues that Truth is not just a passive quality, but the active expression of a True Being</note>; by reason of this, that which is true acts truly. Just as if God were not true, no being could be true. 

It is asked **ninthly** whether God is **glorious**, to which it must be responded affirmatively. Otherwise, no action of God would be glorious or delightful, and thus a **secondary agent** <note>A created being, such as a human or an angel</note> would be more glorious than the **primary one** <note>God, the First Cause</note>, which is clearly false. 

And thus it is clear that there exists one Being in whose existence all things are **converted** <note>referring to the logical "conversion" mentioned on the previous page, where attributes like Greatness and Goodness are interchangeable because they are identical in God</note>, equally according to one most simple **essence** and identity. Let these remarks suffice concerning the first **subject** considered in this **science** <term>science: here meaning a systematic branch of knowledge or "The Art" of reasoning developed by the author</term>, which is interconnected through the whole **spherical figure** titled **"A"** <note>Figure A is a circular diagram used in the "Art of Ramon Llull" to demonstrate how divine attributes are interconnected and equal in the divine nature</note>. 

And it must be noted that the other subjects of this method...

A prior nature is not convertible with a posterior nature. Wherefore, etc. And let these things suffice, for the sake of brevity, regarding the questions of the first figure, which is titled **A.**

## Concerning the questions of the second figure, which is Figure T.

The questions of the second figure can be formulated in three ways, just as each angle of the triangle <note>Figure T in this system is often depicted as a set of triangles representing relational concepts</note> signifies three kinds for us. For example: the **angle of difference** signifies to us the difference between one **intellectual** being and another **intellectual** being. Secondly, between an **intellectual** being and a **sensory** <note>original: "sensuale"; referring to things that can be perceived by the five senses</note> being. Thirdly, between one **sensory** being and another. And for that reason, one must first ask...

<summary>This page explains the logical hierarchy that prevents universal attributes from being interchangeable with specific creatures and introduces Figure T, which categorizes relationships like "Difference" into three levels: purely spiritual, mixed, and purely physical.</summary>
<keywords>Logic, conversion of terms, Goodness, Figure A, Figure T, Hierarchy of Nature, Difference, Intellectual being, Sensory being</keywords>

...most <note>This completes the word from the previous page, likely "intellectual" or "intellectually."</note>, whether God is the distinguishing principle, to which it must be answered in the affirmative. Otherwise, his **dignities** <term>In this system, "Dignities" (dignitates) refer to the fundamental attributes of God, such as Goodness, Greatness, and Wisdom, which are seen as the building blocks of reality.</term> and his actions would be idle within God. And thus an angel would not be distinct from God himself, and so angelic goodness, etc., and the dignities would exist in a state of **confusion** <note>original: "confuso"; in medieval logic, this refers to a lack of distinction or a blurred state where individual identities are lost, rather than "disorder."</note>, not only in the angel, but even in God himself, which is highly absurd.

For it would also follow that the **bonifier**, the **bonifiable**, and the **act of bonifying** <term>These terms (bonificativum, bonificabile, bonificare) are technical Lullian neologisms. They describe how an attribute like Goodness functions: there is an actor (the bonifier), a recipient (the bonifiable), and the action itself (bonifying).</term> of Goodness would have no difference, but would be confused together, which would also be inappropriate; wherefore it is clear that God is the distinguishing principle, and thus God is the cause of the distinction of things.

Furthermore, it is the property of the bonifier to bonify, which cannot occur without a distinction between the bonifier itself and the bonified: for just as acting cannot exist without a difference between the **agent** and the **object of the action** <note>original: "agentis & agibilis"</note>; so also bonifying cannot exist without a difference from the aforesaid; wherefore it is clear that God is the one who distinguishes the **intellective**, the **understood**, and the **act of understanding** <note>original: "intellectiuum, intellectum & intelligere"</note>. Just as Goodness distinguishes the bonifier, the bonifiable, and the act of bonifying from one another; nor does it follow from this that there are multiple goodnesses in God, nor multiple essences which the difference establishes, because l- <note>Catchword for the next page, likely "quia l-" (because...)</note>

<summary>This page argues that God must be the source of distinction; otherwise, the divine attributes would be inactive and the boundary between God and his creations (like angels) would vanish. It introduces the Lullian "ternary" structure, explaining that every attribute contains an active, passive, and verbal component that are distinct yet remain a single essence.</summary>

...produced from both of them, each of which exists in its own number, yet is the same with the others through essence. Otherwise, the **difference** <note>original: "differentia"</note> within them would be confused, and **eternity** would thus fail, for it would not preserve the proper number for each of them. Furthermore, the divine intellect would not know what sort of thing the **bonifier** was, what the **bonified** was, and what the **act of bonifying** was; <term>Lull uses these terms (bonificans, bonificatum, bonificare) to describe the active nature of Goodness. Therefore, etc.

Secondly, it is asked whether **difference** is more universal than **concordance** and **contrariety**. To which the answer must be in the affirmative; otherwise, in those things lacking contrariety <note>That is, things that do not oppose each other, such as different virtues or the different persons of the Trinity.</note> nothing would be distinct, and thus we would be as we were before.

Thirdly, it is asked whether **concordance** <note>original: "concordantia"; the principle that allows different things to agree or work together.</note> is more of a primary principle than **contrariety** itself. To which the answer must be in the affirmative, as is evident: for from concordance descend the **positive principles**, and from contrariety descend the **privative principles**. <term>In medieval logic, a "positive" principle is something that exists in its own right (like light), while a "privative" principle is defined by the absence of something (like darkness being the absence of light).</term> Namely, every positive thing is more worthy to hold the name of a "principle" and to be a first principle than a privative thing; therefore, etc.

Fourthly, it is asked whether every **quidditative definition** <term>A "quidditative" definition (from the Latin "quid," meaning "what") is one that expresses the very essence or "what-ness" of a thing.</term>, in saying that a human is a "humanifying animal," <note>original: "animal homificans"; Lull often redefined species by their characteristic internal action rather than just their "rationality."</note>

<summary>This page uses scholastic logic to argue that formal distinctions must exist within God's unity to avoid "divine ignorance," while also establishing that concordance is a more fundamental principle than opposition.</summary>
<keywords>divine intellect, bonification, concordance, contrariety, positive principles, privative principles, quiddity, Ramon Llull</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the instructions for using the Lullian system of logic. ...[in] divine goodness there is concordance. And it is asked fourthly, what is the good and great justice of God? <note>original: "iustitia dei bona, & magna"</note> And it is asked fifthly, what is the goodness of God, and what is His greatness?

And in the same way, one can ask about "good divine greatness" through the other species of **Rule C** <term>Rule C: In Lull's system, this rule asks "What is it?" (Quid?). For example, by asking: what does the good greatness of God have in itself essentially? And similarly regarding divine difference and concordance; and one must proceed in this way with the other two species of Rule C.

And all these particular questions are solved in the same way as the universal ones, by descending from those universals to their particulars, seeking harmony <note>original: "concordando"</note> and avoiding inconsistencies. This is because no **universal** <term>Universal: A general concept or category that applies to many things (e.g., "Goodness" in general).</term> is opposed to its **particular** <term>Particular: A specific instance or sub-category of a universal (e.g., "the goodness of Justice").</term>, and vice versa. 

I leave out further examples for the sake of brevity. For in whatever way a question is posed concerning divine goodness, greatness, and so on, the solution is drawn from the description of God and from the definitions of goodness, greatness, etc. This is done by harmonizing the definitions and the various parts of the rules, while holding to either the affirmative or the negative side. And this rule is infallible; and just as was exemplified...

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of how the "spaces" or combinatorial cells of the Fourth Figure are used to interpret and prove fundamental philosophical authorities. It moves from discussing God as "pure act" to examining a core principle of physics and natural philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>
<sig>H</sig>

And through the first **E.**, they are necessary. And through **F.**, they are joined and measured infinitely, well, and eternally, and separated from every **accident** <term>Accident: In Aristotelian philosophy, a property or quality that a thing possesses but which is not essential to its nature (for example, the color of a person's hair is an accident, while being a rational animal is their essence).</term>. And thus, having made this exposition, it is clear and open that God is **pure act** in his existence and in his action, etc. 

Furthermore, one reads in **natural philosophy** <note>original: "naturali philosophia"; in the medieval context, this refers to the study of the physical world and the laws of nature.</note> that **nothing comes from nothing** <note>original: "ex nihilo nihil fit"; a foundational axiom in ancient and medieval science asserting that matter cannot be created out of void by natural means.</note>. To explain and declare this authoritative statement, the little space **D. F. E.** is assigned. Through **D.** it is explained that "nothing" is not a **principle** <term>Principle: The source, origin, or fundamental basis from which something else arises.</term>, since if it were, then "nothing" would already be "something," which is an absurdity. 

Moreover, through the rule of **D.** it is shown that "nothing" cannot be the matter for anything that exists; for if something could be made out of "nothing" itself, then "nothing" would already be "something." This same point is understood regarding the third type of the rule of **D.**: if "nothing" were the subject of anything, it would already be something. 

The rule of **F.** signifies that from "nothing," nothing is made, nor can "nothing" be a **medium** <note>In a syllogism, the "medium" or middle term is the link that connects the two premises to reach a conclusion.</note>; for if it were, then it would be something. 

And through **E.** it is understood that "nothing" cannot be a **material cause**, nor an **efficient cause**, nor a **final cause** <term>The Four Causes: Aristotle's framework for explanation. The material cause is what a thing is made of; the efficient cause is the agent that brings it into being; the final cause is the purpose or goal for which it exists.</term>, nor can it be possessed of any power. For if it were, then by the opposite it would follow that it was "something," and thus it is evident...

<summary>This page applies Lull’s combinatorial logic to natural philosophy, demonstrating that the principle "nothing comes from nothing" is logically sound because "nothingness" lacks the essential properties of a principle, matter, or cause.</summary>

For example:

*   **B** signifies **Goodness**, **Difference**, **God**, **Justice**, and **Greed**.
*   **C** signifies **Greatness**, **Concordance**, an **Angel**, **Prudence**, and **Gluttony**.
*   **D** signifies **Duration**, **Contrariety**, **Heaven**, **Fortitude**, and **Lust**.

It should be noted that every particular question contains its own implicit solution, which can be brought back to the **Universal Art** <note>Also known as the *Ars Magna*; Lull's system for organizing all human knowledge into a single logical framework.</note> by following its specific method. Furthermore, what has been said regarding the first mode of multiplying the Fourth Figure applies equally to the remaining modes in their own respective ways.

These are neologisms created by Llull to describe the internal mechanics of a human being's existence.</note> And if it is asked: **to whom does man belong?** To which it must be answered that he belongs to God. And let these things suffice concerning man, whose quiddity <term>quiddity: The essential nature or "what-ness" of a thing.</term> is **man-ness** <note>original: *homeitas*.</note> itself.

The **fifth subject**, whose properties are considered in this science, is the **imaginative power** (or the imagination). Regarding this, it is first asked: **whether it exists?** To which it must be answered in the affirmative. Otherwise, an animal would move by chance, or would be without motion, for the imagination in an animal is the **first mover**. <note>In this context, the imagination is the "first mover" of the body because it provides the mental image of an object that triggers desire and subsequent physical movement.</note> And if it is asked: **what is the imagination?** To which it must be answered that it is a power of the soul whose specific property is to represent to the **thinking faculty** <note>original: *virtuti cogitativae*.</note> those sensory impressions <note>original: *species sensatas*; the "forms" or "images" of things gathered by the five senses.</note> perceived by the senses, or those infused by another power. ### The Fifth Part on the Questions regarding the Essences of the Hundred Forms

Truly, questions concerning the **essences** <term>quiddities (quiditates): The "whatness" or essential nature of a thing.</term> of the hundred forms are generated in as many ways—or can be generated in as many ways—as any given form differs from the nine subjects. 

For indeed, **Essence** is one in one way **formally** <note>original: "formaliter." In medieval logic, this refers to the way something exists according to its own specific definition or "form."</note> in God, and in another way one in an Angel, and in another way one in the heavens, and in another way one in man, and in another way one in the **imaginative power** <note>original: "imaginatiuo siue imaginatiua." The mental faculty of forming images.</note>, and in another way one in the **sensitive power** <note>original: "sensitiuo siue sensitiua." The faculty of sensory perception.</note>, and in another way one in the **vegetative power** <note>original: "vegetatiuo siue vegetatiua." The biological faculty of growth and nutrition.</note>, and in another way one in the **elementative power** <note>original: "elementatiuo siue elementatiua." Relating to the four physical elements.</note>, and in another way one in the **instrumentative power** <note>original: "instrumentatiuo siue instrumentatiua." The capacity to use tools or act as a medium.</note>, and in another way one in **modality** <note>original: "modalitate." The specific manner or mode in which something exists.</note>. 

The result is that these ten **unities** <note>original: "vnitates." These represent the "Ten Subjects" of the Lullian system through which all things are analyzed.</note> are formally distinguished one from another, even though in the essence of unity, each one is "one." For it is just as when it is asked concerning unity, simply and absolutely, whether it is the first principle of all things and of all other unities: to this, the answer must be given in the affirmative. Otherwise, there would be no God, nor would there be number, nor would there be even nothingness. And if it is asked: what is unity in its simplest sense?

<summary>This page introduces the fifth part of the treatise, explaining how a single essence (like unity) is expressed differently across ten distinct levels of being, from God to physical elements. It argues that absolute unity is the necessary first principle for the existence of everything, including God and the concept of number.</summary>

<keywords>quiddity, essence, unity, ten subjects, formal distinction, first principle, God, Lullian logic</keywords>

To which it must be answered, that this is the **essence** of one first principle existing through itself; indeed, this first principle existing through itself cannot be anything other than that which is called God. Consequently, this first principle is nothing other than divine being. On account of this, one must examine its **formality** <note>original: "formalitate." In medieval philosophy, this refers to the specific objective character or "form" that makes a thing what it is.</note>, or its **quiddity** <term>quiddity (quiditate): The "whatness" or essential nature of a thing; that which is described by its definition.</term>, or its abstract nature, which is nothing other than the **essence** of God. Hence, this essence is primary to all other essences—and the same is true of its goodness, its greatness, and its duration—which is not the case for the angelic essence, nor the celestial, nor the human.

And thus the difference between the divine essence and the essence of creatures is clear. Secondly, it is asked whether **being** <note>original: "esse."</note> and **essence** <note>original: "essentia."</note> are **interchangeable** <note>original: "conuertantur." A logical term meaning the two concepts are identical in scope and can be used to define one another.</note>. To which it must be answered that in some subjects they are, and in some, they are not. The first part is demonstrated because otherwise nothing would be equally interchangeable with another, which is impossible. The second part is evident through this: for if essence—or **being** and **entity**—were interchangeable in every subject, then nothing would exist through **participation** <term>participation (participationem): The concept that created things do not possess their qualities (like goodness or life) by their own power, but "participate" in or receive them from a higher source (God).</term>, but all things would be as they are by their own essence; and thus there would be no first supreme good, nor
