...[the] first would be the highest being: and thus it would not be the last, and in this way nothing would exist, which is contrary to the senses <note>The author argues that if the chain of being did not have a logical structure where being and essence align, our sensory experience of the world would be impossible.</note>. Similarly, it is asked whether **one** and **unity**, **simple** and **simplicity**, or **indivisible** and **indivisibility** are equally **interchangeable** <note>original: "conuertantur." In medieval logic, terms are interchangeable if they refer to the same reality, even if the words have different meanings.</note>. 

To which it must be answered that in some subjects they are, and in some, they are not. 

The first part is clear because if it were not so, nothing would be "one" through its essence. Thus, nothing would be found in nature where the **concrete** and the **abstract** are the same being <note>In this philosophical context, a "concrete" term refers to a thing that has a quality (like "something white"), while an "abstract" term refers to the quality itself (like "whiteness"). In humans, these are different; in God, they are one and the same.</note>. For if in nature itself there is found a concrete "one" that is distinct by reason from its abstract "unity," then it is logically necessary that another concrete thing be found which is identical to its abstract form according to both its being and its reason. 

The second part is evident, because otherwise there would be nothing that was distinct from its abstract form <note>Meaning: if everything was identical to its abstract form, there would be no difference between a person and the concept of "humanity."</note>. Similarly, this must be said of the **divine unity**, which is the abstract form of the "divine one." This "divine one" is indeed the same as its own unity. 

Furthermore, this "divine one" is not "nothingness"; it is **being**, **truth**, and **goodness** through infinite truth, through infinite unity, and through infinite goodness. From these is constituted one **Word** <term>Word (verbum): A theological term referring to the "Logos" or the Second Person of the Trinity, representing the expression of God's intellect.</term>, one infinite being, and all- 

<summary>This page explores the metaphysical identity between a thing and its essence, arguing that while created things are distinct from their abstract qualities, in God, the "concrete" and "abstract" (such as the Divine One and Divine Unity) are perfectly identical.</summary>
<keywords>metaphysics, divine unity, concrete vs abstract, simplicity, indivisibility, the Word, infinite goodness, essence of God</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of God's essence, specifically focusing on how the attributes of being, unity, truth, and goodness intersect. It transitions from the previous page's distinction between concrete and abstract forms (such as "one" vs "unity") to the infinite nature of divine unity and its attributes.</meta>

...powerful, which indeed **being** <note>original: "esse." In medieval philosophy, this refers to the fundamental act of existence or the essence of a thing.</note> is understood together with the **one**, the **good**, and the **true** simultaneously, as in this figure, about which figure it was spoken of clearly in the *Little Book of Figures* <note>Likely a reference to a specific treatise on the "Art" of logic or symbolic diagrams, common in the tradition of Ramon Llull.</note>.

-> **Being** <-
**One** | **True**
-> **Good** <-
<note>The diagram shows four circles intersecting. At the top is Being, at the bottom is Good, on the left is One, and on the right is True. This illustrates that in the divine nature, these four concepts are distinct in name but unified in reality.</note>

Concerning the relationship held here, it must again be asked whether it is appropriate for the **divine unity** to unite unto infinity; to which it must be answered affirmatively. Otherwise, the divine unity would be finite, and consequently its goodness, its greatness, its power, its duration, its wisdom, and so on, which are attributed to it; and its **dignities** <term>dignities</term><note>A technical term for the fundamental divine attributes (Goodness, Greatness, Eternity, Power, Wisdom, Will, Virtue, Truth, and Glory) which are considered identical with God's essence.</note> would be bound and not free, and in eternity [they would stand...] <note>The text ends with the catchword "ste-", likely the beginning of the Latin "starent" (would stand) or "stetissent" (would have stood).</note>

<summary>This page explores the interconnectedness of divine attributes, using a symbolic diagram to show how Being, Unity, Truth, and Goodness are unified. It argues that God's unity must be infinite, or else all other divine attributes like wisdom and power would be limited and "bound."</summary>
<keywords>divine unity, Being, Truth, Goodness, dignities, infinity, Ramon Llull, metaphysics, divine attributes</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical argument from the previous page regarding the infinite nature of divine unity. It transitions from discussing how God's attributes (Dignities) cannot be idle to exploring how unity operates in created beings like angels, the heavens, and humans, before returning to the concept of Divine Simplicity.</meta>

...would have stood idly <note>original: "stetissent otiose." This completes the sentence from the previous page, arguing that if God's unity were finite, His attributes would be unable to act.</note>, which is impossible; consequently, it is asked whether it is fitting for **angelic unity** to unite. To this, it must be answered affirmatively, though conditionally: for if one angel unites, it then unites a single moral "speaking," a single "understanding," a single "loving," and a single "worshipping." It does not, however, unite one angel, one heaven, one man, or things of this sort. <note>The author distinguishes between an angel's power to unify its own actions (like thinking or loving) versus the power to create or unify other substantial beings.</note>

But the **heaven**, according to its own unity, is the efficient cause of an infinity of these lower things; yet it cannot unite according to its primeval unity. Rather, it unites through the continuity of motion. Similarly, one man with his unity can unite another man by generating him <note>Referring to biological reproduction as a form of "unifying" or bringing a new being into existence.</note>, and so it must be said of all other unities appropriated to their respective subjects.

And if it is asked whether God is a **most simple nature**, or a **most simple being** <note>original: "simplicissima natura, sive simplicissimum esse." This refers to the doctrine of Divine Simplicity, which posits that God has no parts and is not composed of different elements.</note>: it must be answered affirmatively. Otherwise, there would be no most simple **goodness**, no most simple **greatness**, nor **duration**, and so on for the rest, which is impossible.

And if it is then asked: "Therefore, is there no **plurality** in God?" To this, it must be answered: that this is true if one considers His essence, which is most pure; but it is not the case if one considers His **essential internal correlatives** <term>correlatives</term><note>A key concept in Ramon Llull's philosophy. He argued that every divine attribute has an active, passive, and connective principle—for example, Goodness consists of "The Bonifier," "The Bonified," and "Bonifying." This allows for dynamic action within God without violating His simplicity.</note>...

<summary>This page discusses how different levels of being (angels, heavens, and humans) exercise their own forms of unity through action and generation. It concludes by affirming that while God is "most simple" (without parts), He contains internal "correlatives" that allow for dynamic plurality within His essence.</summary>

<keywords>angelic unity, heavenly motion, generation of man, divine simplicity, plurality in God, internal correlatives, essence</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical exploration of divine simplicity. It moves from the previous page's assertion that God is a "most simple nature" to explaining how this simplicity coexists with the Trinity. It uses Ramon Llull’s characteristic "correlative" terminology (the active, passive, and verbal forms of an attribute) to explain the internal life of God versus the nature of created beings like angels.</meta>

...then it is false: which is because unless there were in God a **bonifier** <note>original: "bonificativum." In Llull’s philosophy, this refers to the active principle of Goodness—the Father.</note> (which is the same as the Father), and a **bonified** <note>original: "bonificatum." This refers to the object or product of Goodness—the Son.</note> (which is the same as the Son), and a **bonifying act** <note>original: "bonificatio." This refers to the shared activity or union of Goodness—the Holy Spirit</note> (which is the same as the Holy Spirit), then God would not be good. 

Similarly and in such a way, it must be answered by conceding that in God there is no plurality when considering His most pure total essence; but when considering the **essential correlatives** <note>These are Llull's "correlatives of being": the doer, the thing done, and the act of doing. He uses these to explain how God can be one essence but three persons.</note> called by another name, we say that the claim [of no plurality] is false. On the contrary, in God there is a **plurality of persons** just as there is a plurality of essential correlatives—which are the **deifier**, the **deified**, and the **deifying act** <note>original: "deitativum, deitatum & deitatio." These represent the active, passive, and connective principles of the Divine nature.</note>. These are formally distinct, although they are the same in one most simple nature. 

Wherefore, etc. Regarding **angelic plurality**, it is not the same, because an angel is another nature distinct from the divine nature—or from its own essence—through a composition of **act and power** <note>original: "actus & potentia." Scholastic thinkers argued that only God is "pure act" (perfectly realized). Angels, while higher than humans, still have "potentiality" (the capacity to change or learn), which makes them composite rather than simple.</note>. These are two unities causing the first number, which is the **binary number** [the number two], as has been declared elsewhere: namely, in the little book or treatise *On the Conditions of Figures and Numbers*, which precedes this little book in order. 

Consequently, it is asked: does **unity** oppose **plurality**? To which it must be answered...

<summary>This page uses Ramon Llull's "correlative" logic to argue that God's unity contains a plurality of persons (the Trinity) through the active and passive functions of His attributes. It contrasts this divine simplicity with the nature of angels, who are inherently "dual" because they consist of both act and potential.</summary>

<keywords>Trinity, Ramon Llull, Divine Simplicity, bonificativum, correlatives, angelic nature, act and power, binary number, Scholastic logic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical defense of fundamental metaphysical categories. Following the previous discussion on the plurality of persons within the divine nature, the text now applies the same logical rigor to establish the reality of basic concepts like nature, substance, and cause. The handwritten numeral 80 next to the printed 82 suggests a discrepancy in early pagination or a specific archival system.</meta>

affirmatively. Otherwise, the opposition between relative terms <note>original: "oppositio in relatiuis." In logic, relative terms (like father and son, or cause and effect) require each other to exist; if one is denied, the relationship collapses.</note> would be destroyed. Consequently, it is asked whether **nature** exists. To this, it must be answered affirmatively; otherwise, no natural being would be found in nature. And it is asked whether **substance** exists, to which it must be answered affirmatively: Otherwise, there would be nothing sustaining **accidents** <term>accidents: properties that do not exist on their own (like the color red or a specific weight) but must belong to a "substance" (like an apple)</term>, or standing under them.

And similarly, we can ask about a **principle** <term>principle (principium): a source or starting point from which something else originates</term> whether it exists, to which it must be answered affirmatively; otherwise, there would be no **principled thing** <note>original: "principiatum." This refers to anything that has a beginning or source.</note>. And similarly regarding a **cause**, whether it exists, to which it must be answered affirmatively. Otherwise, there would be no **caused thing**. In the same way, regarding the **necessary**, whether it exists, to which it must be answered affirmatively; otherwise, all things would be **contingent** <term>contingent: things that could either exist or not exist, and are not strictly required to be</term>, and nothing would happen by necessity, which is contrary to experience. 

And if it is asked whether the **indivisible** exists, it must be answered affirmatively: Otherwise, one would proceed to infinity in dividing and things divided, which is logically unacceptable. And if it is asked whether an **element** exists, it must be answered affirmatively; otherwise, there would be neither **matter** nor **form**, and consequently nothing **formed**, and thus nothing would exist at all. And if it is asked whether the "same" exists, or **identity**, to which it must be answered affirmatively, otherwise...

<summary>This page presents a series of logical proofs for the existence of fundamental metaphysical concepts—such as substance, cause, and necessity—arguing that denying them leads to logical absurdities or contradicts daily experience.</summary>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page concludes a series of logical demonstrations regarding metaphysical categories. It follows the previous discussion establishing the existence of nature, substance, and cause by showing that without these foundational concepts, reality would be an undifferentiated and incoherent mess. This section specifically addresses numerical unity, likeness, order (first/middle/last), and potentiality before concluding the treatise.</meta>

...one would not find a single thing by number <note>original: "vnum numero." This refers to numerical identity—the idea that a thing is one specific, individual thing rather than a vague mass.</note> in the whole of nature, but all things would be confused and indistinct. It is further asked whether **likeness** exists, to which it must be answered affirmatively; otherwise nothing would be uniform, and thus the **relation of equality** <term>relation of equality (relatio æquiparantiæ): a logical relationship between two things of the same status or nature, such as two equal weights or two things of the same species</term> would be destroyed.

And if it is asked whether the **first** exists, it must be answered affirmatively; otherwise there would be neither the last nor the middle, and thus nothing would exist. And if it is asked whether **potentiality** exists, it must be answered affirmatively; otherwise there would be nothing active and nothing passive <note>original: "actiuum & passiuum." In the logic of the time, "active" refers to the power to cause change, while "passive" refers to the capacity to be changed.</note>, wherefore, etc.

And just as I have given examples through the question of **Rule B** regarding these forms, so you should understand that I have done so for the other questions of the rules of the entire **alphabet** <note>The "alphabet" refers to a symbolic system (common in the works of Ramon Llull) where letters represent divine attributes or logical principles to be combined in various ways.</note> by combining all the rules themselves with all the forms of which mention has already been made above in the first part of the third treatise. To these, any **Kabbalist** <term>Kabbalist: here, the author likely refers to a practitioner of "Christian Kabbalah" or Lullian "Kabbalah," which used logical combinations to understand the divine nature</term> ought to recur for a perfect understanding of the **quiddities** <term>quiddities (quiditatum): the "what-ness" or essential nature of a thing; that which makes a thing what it is</term> of all one hundred forms. These questions I leave aside for the sake of brevity, etc.

->THE END.<-

<summary>This page concludes a metaphysical treatise by proving the necessity of concepts like likeness, order, and potentiality, arguing that without them, nature would be indistinct. It ends by directing students of the "alphabet" and "Kabbalah" to apply these rules to all one hundred essential forms.</summary>

<meta>The previous page concluded a logical treatise on metaphysical categories like unity, equality, and potentiality. This page marks the beginning of a new section or volume: the title page for Ramon Llull’s "Ars Brevis," a famous work on logic and universal knowledge, edited by his follower Bernard de Lavinheta.</meta>

### THE ART
# THE SHORT [ART] OF THE ENLIGHTENED
# DOCTOR AND MASTER
## RAMON LLULL.

WHICH is an introduction and a short path for attaining all sciences in a small and brief amount of time, together with the figures <note>These "figures" refer to Llull's famous volvelles—rotating paper wheels used to combine logical categories mechanically.</note> serving that subject matter, as well as the approval of that science. In the editing of this work, let the reader note how most painstakingly <note>original: "castigatissime." This implies rigorous correction and refinement of the text to ensure accuracy.</note> Master Bernard de Lavinheta, the most faithful interpreter of that art, has labored. For if you either subtract or add an element (even a single jot), you change the sum of the entire matter, and you render yourself unfit for the other books of this art which we are about to release shortly in the same field of study.

In the background, a town and hills are visible. The oval border contains the Latin inscription: ONLY HOPE REMAINED WITHIN <note>original: "SOLA SPES INTVS REMANSIT." This is a classical reference to the myth of Pandora’s Box, where Hope was the only thing left after all evils escaped.</note>.</image-desc>

->PARIS,<-
->At the shop of Gilles Gourbin, under the sign of<-
->Hope, across from the College of Cambrai.<-
->1578.<-
